ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e200	Let me begin by thanking the Committee for good cooperation during the examination of this matter. When things start to go a little fast and grumpy, as we say in Trøndelag, it's an advantage with flexible committee members and a positive basic mood. From this point of view, the Church, Education and Research Committee is in a fortunate situation, with many cheerful committee members and a capable and upstanding committee secretary. Norway and the world are facing major changes and shifts in economics, employment, housing patterns, energy and food needs, to mention something. If there's one thing history has taught us, it's that research, development and higher education are among the most important keys to solving these challenges and making the world a better place to live. Much is good with Norwegian research and higher education today. In recent years, the number of publications has increased sharply, doctoral degrees have doubled in the last ten years, and internationalisation has increased both by the withdrawal of several Norwegian students and researchers, and by the entry of several non-residents. We, that's actually the Moser couple, have even brought home a Nobel Prize for the country. Despite the fact that Norway is a small country with few inhabitants, we have a very open economy, where trade and cooperation with other countries are crucial for our and others' prosperity. We're a high-cost country, which can never be the cheapest at all. We can't be the best in everything either, but we can be the best at something, as we've always been – at least for the last 1,000 years. That is why we need to be able to combine investing in the subjects and environments in which we are best placed to succeed by strengthening the quality of welfare production through the important vocational training courses, in particular those related to health and education. Norwegian higher education institutions are mainly not competing with each other in the fight for research funding, researchers and students, but with institutions around the world. The current structure of higher education does not take this into account, and it is not well organised to meet these challenges. There is a large and broad majority in the United Kingdom behind the main points of the communication. Only the centre party has stood outside this broad majority. The centre party will probably come back to this itself, but I can already reveal that the rumors about the death of the college assignment and the professional training are considerably exaggerated. The objectives of the Structural Communication are high-quality education and research, robust professional environments, good access to education and skills across the country, regional development, world-leading professional environments and efficient resource use. These are simple formulations to stand behind, and everyone has been for them for a long time – it is at least difficult to be against them. But it is the concrete follow-up that is important, and mergers are the most important first follow-up, simply because today's organisation of the sector is not appropriate to meet the objectives we have set. It is not a new issue we are discussing here today. Although size is not necessarily the only salvaging thing, it is a fact that a number of fascinations in recent years have pointed to too small and vulnerable disciplines — despite the fact that the smallest environments of privacy considerations have not been evaluated. If the environment is not above a certain minimum size, we cannot expect it to ensure high quality, either today or in the future. In 2008, the Star Island Committee came up with its setting. They showed that many institutions had weak recruitment and failing quality in central education, that processes were moving towards the dissemination of education and training environments, and that they were set up for many small and narrow doctoral programmes. At that time it was not a political will for real follow-up. Five years ago, SAC funds — cooperation, division of labour and concentration — entered the State budget. That's where they've been since, supported by the whole Stortinget. These means may have led to some aggregations, and this has certainly led to some cooperation, but very little division of labour and concentration. The opposition has tried to criticise the process prior to the presentation of the Storing Message, without fully finding out why, at least I haven't understood it. In this context, let me draw the institutions in the sector and how they have met this challenge. Before I got into the Storting, many people said that this sector is characterized by arch-conservatives and – to put it mildly – little changeable actors. It's not my impression after two years here on the house and on the Kuf Committee. Most people have treated this in an exemplary way – they have had good conversations in-house and with each other, taken with employees and students on advice, worked out a course and found one or more partners they want to join or have conversations with, with a view to bringing them together. The majority of the Committee is very concerned that the mergers that are now coming must not be carried out only at the administrative level of each institution. It doesn't have much value in itself. Students and Norwegian student organisations have also been very concerned about this. Today, for example, 25 institutions offer economic and administrative subjects and 19 institutions offer teacher training. Institutions must be given the opportunity to develop their peculiarity and sharpen their profile against what is required to succeed. The crucial thing about mergers is to achieve more real division of labour and more real concentration and closer links between professional environments, and to achieve the multidisciplinary demand for employment and business. Student-active research is also an explicit objective. It will be difficult to achieve without good educational environments being closely linked to strong research environments. One of the means of achieving division of labour, concentration and stronger professional environments, as described in the Communication, is more stringent requirements for the creation of master and PhD programmes. In recent years, we have seen a large increase in the number of education programmes within these two categories, without necessarily being linked to existing high-quality professional environments. It is therefore positive that this should apply not only to new programmes but also to existing ones after a transitional period. This will require all institutions to review their portfolio critically and ask themselves whether it is right to do so or whether there are other institutions that have better prospects for success. The funding of higher education institutions is very important for the outcome. In Norway, we have a stable, predictable and relatively large basic allocation and allocation. We're going to keep having that. Nevertheless, a large majority of Stortinget believe that open competitive areas contribute to the promotion of quality, and that it is therefore natural that the competitive share of the basic allocation increases slightly. A prerequisite for this success is that the funds are released to the environment, not to institutions. I further point out that the funding system has been brought back more detailed in the context of the 2016 state budget. What is also important for the institutions is management. The Government proposes in the message that the Vice-Chancellor and the External Director shall be the main model in the future, versus today's main model with the elected Vice-Chancellor. This is supported by most of the parties in Stortinget. It's maybe this one that's got the most attention outside this house lately. The debate has sometimes seemed as if it would be impossible to choose the principal, and that the principal should be appointed from a dark office in KD's basement or from the basement of Storting. Here, too, rumors are considerably exaggerated. It's the boards to decide if you want to add the principal, or whether you should have chosen the principal. The difference now is that the choice becomes more real, because it's a general majority, more than 50 pst. by the Governing Board, which determines. It's not like that today. So the system is becoming more democratic, not less. Employees and students have, and should still have, a majority of the board of directors. They are with the key, regardless of whether you select the Vice-Chancellor or choose to add the Vice-Chancellor. This isn't the end, it's the beginning. Now we're getting what we've been talking about for years, what a large majority agree on, and what will make the sector more rusty for the competition they're in every single day. I'm looking forward to the continuation.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e240	It is natural to start by thanking the case rapporteur, Sivert Bjørnstad, for the responsibility to sew this setting together. There are complex issues that lie under the structural debate in the university and higher education sectors, and there are many cross-cutting interests, including geographical ones. And in this case too, it is an advantage with as broad a majority as possible – so thank you to Sivert Bjørnstad. An overall committee agrees with much of the description of reality, that R & D education and investment are crucial for Norwegian competitiveness and to successfully solve challenging tasks in society. We point out that the UH sector has a complex societal mission: to contribute to scientific excellence in the pursuit of new knowledge and to train candidates who will function well with their competences. And it's just quality good research in good interaction with the education mission we think achieves the best results. There is also an overall committee that believes that we need institutions with a higher profile and stronger professional environments, and that SAK – cooperation, work sharing and concentration – has been necessary, but that we have not achieved that goal. The Workers' Party supports mergers as an instrument for creating stronger institutions. The institutions that are struggling to get enough students and recruit competent professionals for the positions must do something. For some institutions aggregation will be a proper act, for others committed cooperation is better. That is why it is good that a joint committee also emphasises this very approach. But structural changes are not enough to achieve better quality. The division of labour by trades has been a major issue in the debate, and we are also calling for a debate on the division of labour by tasks. All institutions shall be research-based – there is no doubt about it. But what does that mean? That everyone should be world leaders? That the main task is to run R & D with local or national businesses? To stimulate the researcher himself to become a founder or to cooperate with entrepreneurs? Or to be at the top of training engineers or nurses as well as being excellent at museum development and other research dissemination? Some institutions in Norway are able to do this in some subjects, but not all institutions in all subjects. In addition, we find few institutions on a global scale like high-quality goggles with all of these balls at the same time. Quality is the government's mantra in education and research policy – not very controversial, because we're all too quality. But we see in this big message, as we saw in the long-term research plan, a belief that investing in world-leading research will almost automatically provide better quality of education and more research-based development. I don't see any automation in this. The high quality of research will be an important premise for creating new jobs based on new technologies, for example: – and an important premise for good education. But the path from an discovery to a successful company that makes money is long and fragile and is about far more than being the world leader in research. Similarly, it has not been given that a Nobel Prize winner is needed to increase the quality of teaching. Students who were so lucky to experience two Nobel Lecturers at the same time at the Institute of Economics on Blinders have told of two fantastic economists, one of whom was a glittering mediator, while the other's message was more inaccessible. The social role of the sector is complex. Then the Workers' Party expected a broader and more thorough discussion of what quality meant. Something as essential as finding out what quality of education is is is pushed to 2017. Then, of course, many institutions have already merged. Here, things are done in the wrong order. This also applies to future financing. I find it disappointing that we are not adopting a new funding system today. Instead, we have been given an overview of the possible indicators that the government is assessing, as well as a signal that the results-based part will increase. We don't know how much or how. Instead, this will come in the budget for the harvest, not exactly the place and the room for the great principal balances. They best fit in with groceries messages like the one we handle today. That being said, the Labour Party is well pleased that the government parties on the Stortinget have seen the need for more transparency and transparency about the basic appropriations in the sector. All parties therefore ask the government to return to this with regard to the operation of the basic appropriations under the new financing system. We believe this is necessary in order to get more calm in the sector around this basic allocation, and we are responding to the government's response to this. The government and the majority are in favour of increasing the requirements for being accredited as a university or a higher education institution. The Labour Party is opposed to doing so – a decision of a more principleal nature. There is today a moving transition between higher education, scientific colleges and universities. The clear distinction we used to have between colleges and universities is about to be cleared up. This has been going on over time and is also linked to the debate on funding. The mergers that will now come make this picture even more complex. Small colleges become part of a university at a pen stroke, while institutions that are very close by meeting today's requirements to become a university will experience that the list is being put higher in the middle of the jump. It hardly happens under Bislett Games, to say it like that. The working party believes that it is time for a comprehensive review of the categorisation system and suggests this. At the same time, we can see that many institutions have taken a very high level of professionalism precisely because they have had university ambitions, which is good. But what's worrying is if the traditional college assignment is weakened. For Norway, good professional training is required throughout the country. We will need many newly trained teachers, nurses, protectors, economists, child protection educators, etc. forwards. Then we have to train enough, and we have to train candidates who have relevant research-based competence at a high level. The fact that the Committee underlines just how important this mission is is is that the Workers' Party is very pleased, and I hope that the Council of State will take note of this very thing. One way to acknowledge that understanding is to establish a separate research programme for the professional professions. It will contribute to a necessary quality promise and ensure a balance between good professional training on the one hand and world-leading environments on the other. We both applaud. I'm especially pleased that this message is approaching the debate on leadership in the academy with caution. Management is important, as a unanimous committee clearly states. If you have chosen or hired a Vice-Chancellor, it is now up to the Board to decide, as the case-rapporteur, Mr. Sivert Bjørnstad, well described. And the debate goes. For it is this decision in the vote that has led to the most debate. And I think it's absolutely brilliant, especially when employees and students in the sector are discussing with each other. In short, I would like to point out that there are good arguments for both models, and that we have excellent principals in Norway who are both elected and employed. But for me, it has been essential that with these mergers, the old model of elected principal becomes more difficult, simply because candidates from a smaller institution will obviously have less chance of being elected than candidates from a larger institution. We now see examples of mergers where the big institution wants elected management, it will have less employees. I'm not very surprised that they just take these positions. Finally, a few words about the institute sector. This part of Research Norway has an important role that we want it to continue to play: more emphasis on applied research and an important role in value creation. At the same time, SAK for the institutions is something that should be considered to the greatest extent. The same applies to instruments for better cooperation with universities and higher education institutions. But also here it is about ensuring a good division of tasks and different roles, so that the broad and very complex societal mission of research, development and higher education is best taken care of. Finally, I would like to comment on the final reply to the case rapporteur, Mr Bjørnstad, that we are at the beginning. Well, the start of this merger was probably really when the Council of State Gudmund Hernes started with the Norwegian network and merged very many smaller institutions. What we see now is the continuation of SAK, which the Council of State Kristin Halvorsen started, and got through seven institutions that merged. So this is a step on the way to a slightly different university and college system, and I actually think we're getting closer to the end than we're at the beginning. Then I will address the proposal of the Workers' Party and the proposals of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and SV are agreed.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e274	The representative of Marianne Aasen has taken up the suggestions she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e277	I will also be allowed to thank the rapporteur and the committee for their good cooperation in this matter. Universities, colleges and our research institutes are a large and important area of society. The way we choose to manage our human capital has great consequences for society and for future generations. Much works well in the Norwegian higher education sector, but the sector as a whole is not organised to meet future quality requirements in education and research. In the submission of the work of the Productivity Commission in winter, Mr Rattsø, Head of the Commission, expressed strong concern about several aspects of the Norwegian education sector. He asked questions about our ambitions and asked if it is good enough to be in the middle of the tree. Councilsø referred to the fact that, internationally, we have intermediate achievements in education, research, innovation and the public sector. Is that good enough? No, we don't think this government should tolerate it. In the period 2000 to 2012, international trade panels have carried out a total of 27 evaluations of Norwegian research communities under the direction of the Norwegian Research Council. One of the review themes of several evaluations is that many disciplines are too small to pursue important research issues, and too small to assert themselves in international competition. In evaluations of the education activities carried out by NOKUT, the National Agency for Quality of Education, the panels have also expressed concerns about the many small educational environments. We have many vulnerable professional environments with scattered, small educational facilities. More people are experiencing a failure of recruitment. Some of the institutions have problems attracting professionals, other students. Research and publication efforts vary greatly. Similarly, the ability to raise external funding and the opportunities to participate in international cooperation. These are the challenges we recognise from the report Nou 2008:3 of the Stjernø Committee – New structure in higher education. At the time, the hearing showed that there was a broad consensus on the situation description, but little support for follow-up. One of the reasons for this is that these are key institutions in the economic, cultural, social and regional development of the country, and therefore vulnerable to the different parts of the country. However, if our universities and colleges are to play their part in the future, they must provide high-quality education and research and have extensive cooperation with society and industry. Our students deserve high-quality education at all places of study across the country. They're going to solve tomorrow's challenges. At the same time, we need researchers who can claim in the national and international arena. They need heavy and solid professional environments to work in. This is the background for us today to deal with a big message that not only gives a broad overview of the situation description, but also of the objectives and the action page. The message we are debating needs to be seen in a broad context. The government is now making a major effort on knowledge to strengthen Norwegian competitiveness and equip Norway for the future. We have already adopted a long-term plan for research and higher education aimed at strengthening Norway’s competitiveness and innovation capacity, addressing major societal challenges and developing excellent professional environments. The Government has also developed a strategy for research and innovation cooperation with the EU, which sets out the ambitions for Norwegian participation in Horizon 2020. In government budgets, the knowledge effort has been followed up in both education and research. It's the acidiest thing in the world. Looking at our neighbouring countries in Europe, several other countries have had to concentrate resources and modernise and streamline the university and higher education sectors in order to achieve better quality. The objectives of this structural reform are clear. It is about facilitating high-quality education and research. It is about developing more robust and robust professional environments. It is about providing access to education and competence across the country and contributing to regional development and efficient resource use. Not least, it is about facilitating the development of good professional environments that can compete internationally in world-class. It's in this perspective that we need to see the structural message. The Expert Group on Financing has assessed how the financing system for the sector can be set up, and the Minister of Knowledge has already announced that the next step will be a new report on quality in higher education. Here a stone is laid on a rock. In the analysis of productivity, Rattsø said: «The focus of policy must be shifted from competition in appropriations to evaluation of results and costs.» Today, we have good evidence that the results in the sector vary – not only between the different institutions but also within the same institution. There are considerable differences in quality between different disciplines. That is why, in my view, this communication strengthens the fact that it identifies nine clear quality criteria that will apply to the whole sector, regardless of the nature and size of the institutions. In the debate that has taken place so far since the notification, three areas in particular have been discussed. There are structural changes — the merger processes — that are the financing of the sector, and that is the question of elected versus employed management of institutions. As regards the merger procedures mentioned in the notification, there has been close dialogue between the Ministry of Knowledge and the individual institutions. Decisions shall be taken by the King of the Council of States. I am pleased that the entire committee has joined the government's objective of structural reform in the joint comments in the vote. It is worth noting that the investment in world-leading professional environments should be linked to the areas where we as a nation have the best prerequisites for success. The development of such environments must address both education, research and innovation. These have to be seen in context. There's an important signal in the message. A unanimous committee points out that it is important to continue the efforts on instruments such as the Centres for Research-driven Innovation, the SFI, the Centres for Excellent Research, the SFF, and not least the Centres for Excellent Education, the SFU. In my view, this will be one of the success criteria for the success of the reform. The higher education sector is a major social sector. Good funding is essential for the ability to succeed in improving quality. I am pleased that there was an overall committee that points out in the vote that there will be a strength for the sector with more transparency and transparency about the basic allocation. The Committee asks the government to come back to this in the context of the changes in the financing system in the budgetary process. Funding shall promote the quality of the entire sector, of the disciplines and of the professional studies. Overall, we need to set up funding so that we get the most out of the resources. The Committee has jointly pointed out that universities and colleges have historically had different framework conditions in terms of research time and recruitment positions. This is reflected in different basic funding for the so-called old and so-called new universities and colleges. An overall committee asks the government to address this issue and to return to the Storting in an appropriate manner. That's good. Finally, I would like to comment on the management models discussed in the communication. Our universities and colleges manage large resources for the community. It's demanding. Changes in the sector have led to greater competition, including internationally. In addition, institutions are becoming more and more complex. This requires good implementation, governance and management. Although the government is now adding to the notification a new main model with an employed principal and external director, it is clear that the institutions themselves are making the choice for their university or college. It is the Board of Directors which is the top organ of the institution and which has to decide how best to ensure that the managers recruited are the most qualified to take up the task. I support the resolution on the new main model, but I am pleased that it is so clearly emphasised that it is the governments that are taking the final decision. This is important in order to ensure the autonomy of institutions. Good objectives and strategies are important, but results are generated in their implementation. ‘The proof of the pudding is in the eating.’ A good plan must now be implemented with commitment and commitment.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e343	Our country is dependent on good education and high-quality research, and we are going to have high ambitions for the Norwegian university and higher education sectors. The size has adopted the first long-term plan for research and higher education. There we agreed on some long-term bets and clear priorities for the future. We commit ourselves to stepping up the research effort. We fixed a goal of making R & D 3 pst. of GDP by 2030. We agreed to bet on fields such as marine and maritime research, on climate, environment and environmentally friendly energy and on enabling technologies, and we agreed on a goal of developing more professional environments of excellent quality. While we need the spearheads, we also need good professional training across the country. We need good training for future teachers, nurses, engineers, economists, etc. I feel that there is a broad political consensus that we need to make certain structural changes in the sector in order to achieve these objectives. Several reports point out that we have too many small and vulnerable professional environments in Norway, and we may not have optimally structured the UH sector to maximise the investment we make in the sector. I think that's why it's good that there are now a number of positive processes going on. I don't think mergers are the only charity, but I think that in many places it will be necessary and positive. I would therefore commend the government for starting this work, and not least the various institutions that have taken the challenge seriously, and set in motion a comprehensive work. At the same time, on several occasions I have warned against merging institutions against their will. I think a merger will have the best assumptions for success if the parties see a mutual benefit of the merger and want such a process. I am therefore pleased that the Committee expresses the view that the Ministry should not exclude institutions that can meet future quality requirements as independent institutions through commitment cooperation with others from being able to do so. I am also pleased that an overall assessment of the institutions, based on the quality criteria, will be carried out and that emphasis will also be given to matters such as student involvement in research, student satisfaction and follow-up of the students. The Stortinget now opens up so that institutions can change the category again. This means that several of today's colleges will be able to apply to become universities. At the same time, we are tightening up the accreditation requirements. I think this is a reasonable grip. It is no longer the category belonging that is the natural distinction in the division of labour between different institutions. Both universities and universities today offer both professional and discipline studies. They are subject to the same law and the same funding system. I also believe that a university status of those institutions that meet quality requirements can help to strengthen the recruitment of both good students and good researchers from abroad and thus help us achieve some of the national objectives that we have set ourselves for the sector. At the same time, I am concerned that we should have a division of labour in the sector. Everyone must not try to become like the University of Oslo or NTNU. Different institutions have different roles and roles. That is why we need a funding system that facilitates this and that contributes to professional and profile excellence at the institutions. I am also concerned that we should have a transparent funding system. There is a large-scale government that grants resources to the institutions, and it is these appropriations that have made a significant contribution to possible imbalances in the system, but we now need a better understanding of how the basic component is composed. More transparency and transparency will be a strength for the sector, and the Committee is therefore asking the Ministry to come back to this in the context of the changes in the funding system. At the end of the day, a few words about governance and management: The question of elected or employed Vice-Chancellor has created a public debate. I think there are good arguments for both. That is why I am pleased that it is still up to the individual institution to choose this, and that the freedom of choice actually becomes more real after the change that is now taking place.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e366	The desire to develop and improve the higher education sector is no new desire. Over many years, there has been a dissatisfaction with our universities being too low on international rankings, including in Europe. Low counting on institutions' work on repatriation of European research funds has also been subject to frustration. One might apologize with many, but it's up to us that we don't want to be moderate, sail if most of us are. There is a lot of positive drive, however, in the desire for improvement. There has also been a dissatisfaction with the fact that the quality of the higher education sector has not been good enough. Several have pointed out that small devices make environments vulnerable and offer less opportunities for good professional immersion. The quality reform was launched in 2004, but after ten years the changes made at that time do not seem to have had the effect you wanted, and many have shown good progress. Single institutions have improved, but students in general have not become more satisfied. But when part of the back ground for this case is dissatisfied with the colleges, it's interesting to note that college students actually seem to be more satisfied than university students. In 2008, the Starø report was presented. It would look at whether the structure of the sector was forward-looking for, among other things. increased internationalisation. The answer from the sample was: aggregation of institutions. The response from the red-greens was: more committed cooperation, division of labour and concentration. The sector has taken on SAK, but to a lesser extent has made it possible to fulfil its objectives. Thus the blue-blue government, with the Left and Christian People's Party, screws the screw to: the institutions are to be merged. I have said in the past that I believe the government and the Council of State have started to increase the quality of the UH sector in the back. We want higher quality, universities need to go up on international rankings and become more internationalised, and the higher education sector needs to research more and train teachers, nurses and engineers in the wider world of work regionally. But to increase the quality of the sector, we need to think about what quality of higher education is. I can only look at parts of this discussion as something schizophrenic. On the one hand, the distinction and pointing up as the main objective. At the same time, institutions shall continue to meet the needs of regional labour for skills and the business needs for innovation and cooperation with the academia. In spite of this, the fundamental discussion of the mission of the various institutions has been absent in the message and in the political discussion. The majority have pointed out that in 2013 NIFU and IPED were assigned to evaluate quality in higher education. Their work shall be carried out in 2016. The rapporteur was also concerned about the same. That was a good grip. The problem is that the grantor — the government and the state council — does not see the point of waiting for this work until they make structural changes. The Government considers that the answer to the question of increased quality is that institutions are merged and have joint management. The government does not take time to review and analyse the quality drivers. They've made up a set of nine criteria that are their definition of quality. Once the answer has been given in advance, we in the Centre Party are very concerned that the whole process will lead to a large-scale rehabilitation of good trade unions, and that both the quality of education and research and geographical access to higher education will be weakened. We are therefore critical of the starting point and the premise of the process that the government has initiated. The central party believes that the message of one-sided emphasis is on some weaknesses in today’s university and higher education systems, without balancing it with the Norwegian UH institutions actually achieving excellent education and research. We believe that the government in this way underlies the value of knowledge development and candidate production in a variety of institutions spread throughout the country, and the significance of this. The Joint University and Higher Education Act has helped to erase the lines of distinction between the traditional higher education assignment and the university assignment. We see that the government does not have a conscious higher education policy in this. Everyone is not going to do the same thing, but at the same time they're going to do the same thing anyway. And everyone has to be "excellent". It's a pious goal, but colleges have a broader goal than answering the big questions of the world community. They shall also ensure equal access to education and competence throughout the country. Since the government in practice believes that the university is the only answer, the Centre Party fears that the result could be a lower quality in the broad vocational training courses and a lower priority for institutions to meet the need for regional labour. We stand before the great 'high school death'. The country's colleges and universities have been under strong pressure for a year and a half to find merger partners. Sick if some institutions wish to join a larger unit, several of the UH institutions have concluded in the process that they will be best served to continue as maritime institutions. Despite thorough investigations by the institutions, however, the government has chosen to review these professional and strategic assessments by imposing new rounds on them. We believe that such a review is unwise and would rather criticise the government for not allowing to consider alternatives to mergers in the preliminary rounds, but we are very pleased that the committee is instructing the government to do so in the future. It is important that educational provision is distributed to meet the needs of all parts of the country. The government does not propose a reduction in the number of study places. Nevertheless, the Centre Party is concerned that the impact on the long-term can be precisely centralised in the provision of study services. The government cannot guarantee that merged and maritime institutions will gradually make changes to the supply structure in order to bring together professional environments that could lead to the centralisation of the campus structure. It will be up to the board of directors of each institution to decide on the location of the campus and the establishment and closure of study facilities. The centre party fears campus branches without professional qualifications that are fully subject to faculties at other places of study. When the decision-making authority is moved further away from today’s study sites, it could in the long term weaken the local and regional governance of the offers. We therefore believe that the Stortinget must decide if the professional qualifications of the individual campus should be terminated. It is necessary to obtain funding if the government will return to this in the State budget. There is a great weakness in the message that it does not address this, and again shows that the whole reform is an urgent reform. Here you start with the answer – that is aggregation – to explore funding as a result of it, and you end up looking at what quality is. It's really in the back! It is quite understandable that several higher education institutions are attracted by the possibility of better funding, greater autonomy and higher status by becoming part of a university. Access to more professional competence is, of course, very important, but mergers cannot be a prerequisite for doing so. Cooperation between institutions is absolutely necessary and must not be confined to taking place within a region or restricted nationally. We need both national and international cooperation. The centre party is concerned that someone is counting on the gains they see they are now going to achieve. We have examples of this when the new universities were categorized from college to university. How we finance universities and colleges is very important for how the UH sector organizes and how they have the opportunity to develop. The basic component is a recurring theme when institutions address their challenges and needs. The current calculation of the basic appropriations is based on old and outdated policy priorities. The basic component must be more transparent and fairer, and I refer in this respect to the proposals put in by the Central Party and promote them. We are also critical of increasing the output component. There is no fault in itself, but if this is to be a criterion, it must be followed up with clear criteria for premiums also by the institutions which train for the wider working life. The centre party believes that it is disappointing and alarming that the biases are proposed further. We do not want a situation in which institutions with low basic appropriations will have a weaker strategic space for action and poorer opportunities to cut the quality indicators optimally than institutions that have been allocated a high basic component for historical reasons. We expect the government to rectify this when they deal with the financing of the state budget. We are also concerned that the costs of the merger process itself and the weighting of quality requirements in favour of excellent research will divert resources from teaching. There is also a concern that it is the students who have to pay the bill for government ambitions. It is surprising that neither the government nor the committee seems to be particularly concerned about this. The merger process at NTNU is already marked, and a representative from the University of Tromsø, who is already in this process, says that bureaucracy has increased and that the system and rules have become more rigid. We see that there is a majority for the coercion of universities and colleges, but the government also believes that the work in the back is starting here: centralization is the most important, regardless of what you think you get out of it.

We need to create a funding system that allows the different institutions to improve what they're good at, and so that not everyone has to have the breadth of our wide universities. I'm glad that the government is now addressing the problem that goes on at the traditional universities and the new universities and colleges.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e446	The Committee agrees on a great deal in this sector. It is also in the configuration. At the same time, there is a greater and deeper disagreement about what actually creates good research and higher education. Government policies are based on a belief that greater is the best, that more competition leads to higher quality, and that cities are the place where research is primarily to be done. What is most troubling in the proposal for a new government funding system is that they announce that the fundamental freedoms are to form a small part of the funding of the institutions, that competition should mean more. It worries me because innovative, groundbreaking, great research requires long-termity and foresight in the vows. There shall be room to fail and to widen strategic bets over a longer period of time. Most of the researchers' time must be spent on research, teaching and dissemination, at least on other tasks, such as searching for money or adapting research programs to search for money. Kari Melby, Vice-Chancellor at NTNU, wrote this in the Vice-Chancellor's blog last fall: ‘But it is an important point that our ability to produce world-leading environments cannot be based primarily on such a targeted instrument. The main instrument is the basic allocation. Long experience has taught us that if we are to be able to underpin environments with the potential to be world leaders, we need a framework allocation where not all possible purposes are earmarked." Something similar says Edvard Moser, who received Norway's first Nobel Prize in Medicine last year. In a debate in the morning paper he said: ‘The problem is that publishing large quantities is incompatible with publishing as best as possible.’ We should have gone the wrong way, as SV suggested in the budgetary process last year, towards greater basic funding. We will do that because we want autonomous, independent institutions within higher education, not because higher education should be disconnected from society, but because autonomous institutions are better for society. Universities and universities are not producers of study credits or research articles; they are important social actors with critical assignments. With this message we take a step towards a more market-oriented business model for higher education. He builds on the idea that competition provides the best research, that larger units provide the best research, and that more measurement, more quality indicators and reporting provide more scientific breakthroughs. We don't think so, nor does Edvard Moser think so. The number problem that Moser points to is also reflected in the other quality criteria that the Storing Message provides. The category «searching for study programmes» may become a marketing driver that does not correspond to the actual content of the study. Of course, the category ‘high performance’ is good, but not if it becomes a driver of less ambitious study and lower requirements. The category ‘PhD size’ is not a favourable criterion if this means that the institutions are increasing the programme without improving the follow-up of the scholarship holders. New knowledge and research are developing best through a running, open and democratic debate. It is through this openness and the internal democracy of research that new knowledge is discussed and often debated. The basics of this openness and this democracy lie the autonomy of the researchers – that they should not be over-directed by other external relations. Therefore, strengthening the basic funding is so important for SV. Hence, we are skeptical of aggregations with coercion and closure of small subjects because they are small. So we're going to change the model where the principal is the starting point of the academy. There is too much faith that structure is the answer to the challenges in the UH sector. Merging can have a good effect, but it's not a miracle cure. It's not like the students at Gjøvik suddenly wake up to a whole new study day on the day they go to NTNU and the principal in Trondheim. Big doesn't have to be good, small doesn't have to be bad. SV does not mean this system leads to different institutions adapting their mission, and we are therefore too critical to much of the government's proposal. I would like to address the proposal we are making in the vote.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e475	The representative of Torgeir Knag Fylkesnes has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e478	The government has presented a big message of structural reform in the university and higher education sectors, and we have taken on board some major challenges that both of us as a society face: climate change, the combination of lifting millions of people out of poverty while doing so in a sustainable way, the need for change in energy use, and here at home challenges related to the age structure of the population, cracking the welfare community – and the very need to continue to have a strong welfare society and high value creation. It's not new that the world is changing, but the pace may be faster than before, and it'll always be uncertain what it's changing to. Universities and colleges play a key role in our society. They are carriers in the further development of the knowledge society to provide education and education. It's very good to say about today's university and higher education sector, but like most of the other major messages, this is also about the challenges. But I also want to stress that this is not a message that will solve all the challenges. It addresses the overall framework, the structure, and identifies the changes that should be made to it. A considerable amount of resources is used in the university and higher education sectors, but resources are distributed too thinly. There are many small and vulnerable trade unions, including other speakers, and many scattered, small training facilities with failed recruitment. It's not a diagnosis that came up with the new government. In 2008, the Star Island Committee presented an investigation that said the same thing. Evaluation by the National Agency for Quality of Education (NOCUT) also shows the concern that resources are being distributed too much. Under the direction of the Norwegian Research Council, international trade panels have carried out almost 30 evaluations of Norwegian research environments. A review theme is that many professional environments are too small to pursue important research issues, including in the professional field, and to be able to compete in international competition. That is the important starting point for the notification and for the changes we think it is necessary. The discussion on structure in the higher education sector is not new. I've mentioned the Star Island Committee. In the wake of it, the previous government invited the institutions to come together and merge. The majority allocated money for cooperation, work sharing and concentration, and there were some results, but not enough. The government is responsible for the overall structure of the higher education sector, and there is a need for greater changes than we have seen so far. That's why we take responsibility for it and implement changes, and we do it through a process that is involved. This involves good dialogue with the institutions themselves, although not all institutions necessarily get the solution they have at the top of the wish list if they only look at their own institutions. A good process also involves political anchoring. The policy of higher education and research, at least the major structural interventions, has been marked by broad consensus. It's important, because we want solutions that stand over time, and that the Storting ends up on. There is also an important background for the presentation of this message. Quality is the overall objective of the government’s knowledge policy, and the communication of structures expresses clear objectives on high-quality education and research. We're going to have robust professional environments. A strength with the Norwegian system is that we have good access to education and skills across the country, and we still need that. Institutions shall be better able to play an active role in regional development. We want good education at all levels and at all places of study, but we also want environments that claim to be completely at the top of the world. Larger trade unions and larger institutions are helping to do this. We will also focus on efficient resource use, without thinking that bigger will always be better, and there have been good examples here of institutions that are not big in the meaning of many students, but which are nevertheless excellent in their fields of study. It is also important to stress that aggregation is not an end in itself; it is a means of promoting quality. It also means that neither the institutions nor the government nor the Storting are done when mergers are carried out. Quality is a goal we must constantly work for, and an important part of that work will be a key message of quality in higher education in 2017. In the Structural Communication, we point to some criteria that say something about quality or how robust an institution is: the number of years of first-rate competence, student recruitment, the time-use and completion of students, the publication of new research, external revenues, the size of doctoral programmes, interaction with society and international orientation. These are not new criteria; they are known criteria that have over time been used by both the sector, politicians and others to discuss quality. It is also the most relevant criteria for assessing structure. Therefore, it makes sense to use them as a starting point to assess whether an institution is solid enough to stand alone. But there's no formula for quality, and there's no formula for structure either. In other words, we do not set any absolute minimum requirements or evaluate solely according to the criteria. We need to make an overall assessment of the institutions and the alternatives available. However, when someone scores consistently low, it is our opinion that they should be merged with others. More research-based vocational training and the introduction of five-year teacher training are also an important background for structural changes. Many institutions will not have professional assumptions for, for example: to be able to offer a five-year teacher training alone. New recording requirements may also affect recruitment. In the message, we're sketching some institutions that can be merged quite quickly. These assessments are the result of a rather long and very open process. We also point to some institutions that have not yet found their place. We want to have further dialogue with these institutions through a still open process. So there's no secret map now drawn up by the drawer. Other institutions will be doing fine by themselves, but have an important job to do with prioritising, consolidating and developing professional environments within their own institutions. Still others may need to continue the process when they see how the sector evolves and how it will affect them. As I said, aggregation is just one instrument of quality. In the Communication, we also present some other instruments that work together with structural changes, such as: new requirements for master and doctoral programmes to ensure that they are anchored in stronger disciplines. It should still be possible for an institution, for a college, to become a scientific college or university, and that dynamism has also brought with it much good. However, this has also led to the establishment of some predominantly narrow doctoral programmes with few candidates. That is why we are tightening up the criteria for changing the category of institutions. Good governance and management are essential for high-quality education and research. Today, institutions may choose whether to elect the Vice-Chancellor of the Board of Directors or the Chairperson of the External Board and the Vice-Chancellor of the Vice-Chancellor. That choice will remain there, but the government believes that the model of external directors best facilitates both the cleanliness and the recruitment of the most qualified management, and gives the board a clearer and more independent role in the day-to-day management of the institution. Not to say that there are not many very good principals who have been elected, but that is the political, principled decision and the angle of incidence. If the institutions themselves disagree, they will still be able to decide that they should have elected the Vice-Chairman. The change in the UH sector needs to be seen in the context of the large government’s commitment to research and higher education. It must be seen in the context of the long-term research and higher education plan, it must be seen in the context of the budgets, and it must be seen in the context of the quality notification. The notification also refers to other measures, such as: the development of the supervisory model of NOKUT. We have also announced changes to the funding system, based on advice from an expert group and on the process and dialogue with the sector. Some of these adjustments could have budgetary effects. That makes me think they belong in the state budget. Some of the adjustments are so closely related to the structure and institutional framework conditions that we have presented them in the communication. We're going to do the best we can to add up to a process that will give Storting the time to discuss a funding system as well. For the government it has been important to give institutions a predictability by already saying that we want to continue the main features of today's system, while at the same time increasing the profit-based funding over time. This does not mean that basic appropriations are radically reorganized, but that means that over time more will come as a result of competition in open arenas. The size of the performance-based part and the indicators to be included, the establishment of the strength ratio between them and the issues of the open or closed framework have economic implications and should therefore be addressed in the context of the budget.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e630	There is a lot of positive in the message, and there is agreement on a lot. However, there are some themes that have not been given their legal place. It concerns the definition of quality, the question of funding, and it concerns the students – those who are going to study at the institutions, who are going to meet researchers and teach every day, those who live in student homes if they are so lucky that they have a place, those who are sitting over books and lecture notes, and who hopefully also have time for a beer at the student pub. The role of students is underestimated and under-communicated in what has been presented by the government so far. Study quality is, in reality, in the second division of the long-term research and higher education plan, and the topic is scarce in the structural message. From the study barometer to NOCUT, we know that the follow-up, guidance and feedback from professionals is the point most students are dissatisfied with. We also know that 42 pst. of the students who started studying in 2001 had not completed a degree after 10 years. Study quality must be a requirement for all institutions and one of the criteria on which they are measured. If the institutions are not working on making good courses of study for the success of the students, they should be given a strong boost from accumulation and government. There must also be a good link between research and education. Government is concerned with world-leading environments and better research quality. We'll join in, but that's not enough. Yes, research quality is a basis for good study quality, but there is no guarantee of good study quality. Students have little benefit from coming to excellent research environments if knowledge is not disseminated through good teaching arrangements. It simply has little value for students to have world-leading researchers on campus if they never put their legs in a lecture hall, don't care about education or ever talk to a student. It is not that good research automatically becomes good teaching. We see from both the study barometer and the non-response statistics that it alone does not last. What we should do more is, to put it simply, to send researchers more out among students and students more in to the researchers. We need to ensure that students get insight into R & D work and that we develop the capacity for critical and independent thinking, that they are motivated to stay up-to-date after completion. It presupposes that students meet high demands, but also that they receive follow-up, guidance and feedback to meet their demands. This should have been clarified in the long-term plan and the structural communication, we believe. As a society, we have to make high demands for the students, but we have to make it possible to meet those requirements as well. It's reasonable that we give students proper follow-up, and that we give them an economy that allows them to spend more time on the reading room and less on the part-time job. Therefore, the Workers' Party will continue to propose 11 months of study funding and more student housing. But I'll also finally say that it's smart to use their money smartly. We will therefore warn against the proposal of the expert group on turbos. Although it is not part of this message, it may be on the stairs. If the Minister of Knowledge can afford to spend $12 billion. To reward those who already get it, there is a terrible misappropriation of community money. For $12 billion. not only eleven but twelve months of study funding can be introduced. You can raise the scholarship to 1.5 G, or you can build 4,200 student homes for full-time. The role of centres in the Norwegian educational flora is unclear both in terms of organisation and economy. This may mean that not all the institutions offering teacher training today will be able to continue, and therefore teacher training is one example of why it is important to make structural changes. Structural changes are, of course, not sufficient to ensure quality and sustainability. Nevertheless, a number of challenges are linked to the fact that several of our environments are too small, little research and publication, they do not claim to be sufficiently international, and they simply cannot provide the education the country's students need. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e939	First, I would like to thank the rest of the Committee for its constructive work on this matter. The proposed treatment refers to three main areas. The first concerns the introduction of an authorisation to establish regulatory competence requirements for all teachers, i.e.: no longer grant exemptions to teachers trained before 2014. The current derogation is due to the fact that, as is known, teachers who have passed a new scheme are automatically qualified according to the competence requirements. The proposal provides for the retroactive application of the competence regulation, so that all teachers who have been trained before 2014 have to take additional training in order to gain full teaching competence. While an overall committee shares the government's ambition to raise the level of competence in school, the Committee is divided in its view of the proposed change and its consequences. I refer here to the setting, where the parties explain their views and suggestions. The central party shares the government's view of the need for action to increase the professional competence of the school – absolutely! At the same time, we believe that there is a danger that the legislative changes will have adverse consequences, which we do not believe is the answer to what we want to win. In some cases, minor schools may have major problems in meeting the competence requirements for education and helping to press for school closure. More stringent skills requirements will further increase the need for further training of teachers, which in turn will lead to an increased need for temporary workers, who often lack relevant skills, and, paradoxically, far less formal competence and real skills than trained teachers, with perhaps 20 years of experience in the classroom, who are now found to be too easy or unqualified. This way the school will have more unqualified staff in education over several years than today. It is serious when several hearing bodies point out that such a change would adversely affect the protection of teachers' position, and there is a danger that more teachers will turn away from school. There is also a proposal to clarify which decisions in the field of child protection can be taken on behalf of the child when child protection has taken over the care of the child. The Committee supports the Government's proposal. In particular, with regard to decisions relating to the professional and social development of the child, it is important to allow those who are most likely to care for the child’s interests to take the decision. The Committee agrees that these should be the main care of the child and, at the same time, allow parents to decide on the issue, which is a central part of the parental right. The third element of the proposition is the proposal to change the name of the RLE subject to Christianity, religion, life and ethics, KRLE, as well as the warning of a stronger regulation of the use of time in the subject, so that about half of the teaching time in the subject is used for Christian knowledge. As a basis for the proposals, the government refers to the strong position that Christianity has in Norwegian history, and wanted to place greater emphasis on this religion in teaching than today. For the same reason, you want to change the subject's name. As the vote shows, there is a great deal of disagreement about this proposal. The centre party believes that the proposal is unfortunate, may create new uncertainties about the content of the subject and may lead to more parents choosing to apply for exemption from their children. We think it would be very unfortunate if the government's symbolism policy led to students splitting up into a subject where there is a particular need to serve as a pooling and common meeting place for knowledge sharing, discussion and reflection. The regulation of time use will also mean that the government and the majority override the organisation of teaching by teachers and schools – contrary to the intentions of the Knowledge Promise, where the curricula are to be guided by curriculum objectives and not finely distributed on the number of hours and pages of the textbook. It is worth noting that among the hearing bodies there is a great deal of concern about the proposals. Of the 73 hearing bodies that have expressed their opinions on the proposals, a total of 40 warn against the changes, including central bodies linked to the Church and Christian activities. I also refer to the comments of the Central Party in the setting, where the party's views on the matter appear.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e975	Thank you to the rapporteur, who has maneuvered the Committee through a case that contains three different main areas. Many of us say that the teacher is the school's most important resource, and we say that because we agree that it's true. For the Workers' Party, this is primarily about having enough teachers, enough teachers who have time to give their children good learning experiences. But the teachers are not anyone; the teachers are educators and academics. Being a teacher is a profession that a person learns, but the teaching profession is also a profession that a person develops in. You learn as long as you live – a somewhat sweaty expression, but no less true for that reason. One learns through both continuing and continuing training – which we have worked on together and across policies – and through learning in more informal channels, through the working practices that learn, through course, through peer-based learning, and through being generally awake in view of the development of the subjects in which it is taught. It is difficult to measure teacher competence only in terms of study scores, but when we decided in 2012 that teachers should have competence in the subjects they teach, we measured it for one reason – it was about ensuring that teachers had a professional platform to build on. In view of this, we support in the proposal that the competence requirements apply to all teachers who teach in the defined subjects. However, we do not agree that the requirements should only be met by taking a higher education course with credits. Here we believe that the government has a narrow focus on competence. For this reason, we have proposed that teachers with teaching skills should be given the opportunity to be evaluated on a real basis in order to meet the new requirements. In our view, the proposal from the civil parties that we do not want to rule out the possibility for teachers to take examinations without further training is too vague, not in one word. We will ask the government to follow closely how the new requirements work out there, not primarily in relation to the study score development, but in relation to whether they cancel out. Therefore, the 10-year period set for the fulfilment of competence requirements is not the most important, but rather the ability we have to assess and adjust the efforts and mechanisms of the en route requirements over the 10-year period, so that as much as possible falls well under the requirements. So over to the proposals to reintroduce the K in the RLE subject and clarify the management of the time use in the subject. Actually, you don't have to do this much, because rarely has a case been clearer, rarely has the advice from the professional community been so unambiguous, rarely has the historical development shown more clearly what is the right way. After ten years of unrest about religious education in school, we have now calmed down. For the Workers' Party, it is essential that this calmness be maintained. Today, there is an acceptance and common understanding of the subject in almost all camps, and remember, we went from a situation where many sought exemption from a schooling profession, to the fact that everyone now feels included. And much of the reason for applying for an exemption a few years ago, was just about elements of what the government now wants to reintroduce. I would like to emphasize most strongly the value of the fact that there is trust and common understanding around a subject that is just designed to create trust and understanding. I also want to warn against a practice in which we from Stortingets go in and detailed control how much time each teacher in each classroom will spend teaching in different parts of a subject. There's a reason why in this hall we don't say that a teacher has to use 30 pst. of natural science lessons in the 9th class of biology, 30 pst. on physics and 40 pst. on chemistry. We adopt the overall objectives, and the teachers themselves must be allowed to determine how they spend time in their class in such a way that as many of the students as possible reach the goals that we have defined. The Labour Party is therefore – like parents, teachers, trade unions and I think I dare say the vast majority of social voices – to agree that this is an unwise proposal. I will take up the suggestions made by the Workers' Party together with others in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1066	Now we're dealing with a proposition that contains several different suggestions, and I'll first say a little bit about the proposed changes in the RLE subject. These changes are based on the introduction of a national provision for Christianity to be about half the subject. There has been a provision in the past that Christianity should be 55 p.m., but the red-green government removed this provision. At that time, the objectives of competence in the subject were not changed, and the then Minister of Knowledge, Bård Vegar Solhjell, of SV, was clear that there would still be a need to spend as much time on Christianity as before. Even now, we don't change the goals of competence in the subject, but we're re-entering a rule that's more rounder than the old one, that Christianity should be about half. It has been a broad political consensus that Christianity should have a greater share of the subject than other religions, and it is simply about the importance that Christianity has had for the development of the society in which we live. Knowledge of Christianity is an important key to understanding Norwegian society. The proposed changes in the subject do not imply a change in the nature of the subject. There is no need for the subject to be informed in any way or for the training of faith to be returned to the school. RLE, or KRLE, as it is now called, should still be a knowledge-based orientation subject, and it should be a common subject where students with different religious backgrounds should be able to meet and learn about their own and others' beliefs, different life views and ethical reflections. Knowledge of faith is important for building understanding and respect between people. Therefore, it is important that teachers too have knowledge of faith and outlook on life. I would therefore like to take this opportunity to mention also the consensus between the majority of the parties that KRLE subjects are to be introduced as a compulsory part of teacher training, as part of the common education and learning profession. This will help all teachers in the future to have a basic knowledge of the field, and hopefully an increased understanding of the students they are going to work with. This is becoming increasingly important in a school where the student population is becoming increasingly multicultural and multireligious. With regard to the change of name, I believe it is natural that the subject is given a name that reflects more closely the content of the subject. That is why we are pleased that the Stortinget has strengthened the demands for professional immersion for newly educated teachers, and we also believe that the focus on continuing education is positive, and that it will contribute to a level of competence among the teachers who have been employed for a long time in school. However, it is problematic to require that a teacher who has completed an officially approved education, who has received an official approval stamp to be able to teach at school, and who has worked in the job for many years, be able to receive further training in order to continue his job. I think it's fundamentally problematic, I think it's an economically unreasonable priority, and I fear that it will help some good teachers lose their motivation and choose to leave the teaching profession. And that – I mean – we can't afford it. Although it should be possible to make provision for each school, it is not certain that it will mean so much for the individual teacher. And if a math teacher at a high school who has been granted a dispensation has a desire to change employer and work at another school instead, it may be natural that the new employer will look first and foremost at candidates who meet the formal requirements, so that they do not operate on a dispensation. This could therefore weaken the mobility of teachers, and that is not good. Nor does the proposal take into account the fact that a teacher who has worked for many years at school in teaching a subject has gained a lot of real competence. The representative, Thinning Bjørnø, said that you learn as long as you live, and even said that it was a bit of a sweaty word. It may not be much less sweaty to say that you learn as long as you have students, but I think that's also true. I therefore believe that if these requirements are introduced, provision should be made for teachers who have been trained before 2014 to have an opportunity to have their actual competence assessed and, if necessary, an opportunity to take an examination — without participating in the education. And then the proposal that we're behind is already taken up.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1096	I'm glad we've got a proposition that's as clear as this in terms of the desire to strengthen the teacher's competence. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1109	RLE is an important subject. In addition to learning about the great world religions of Christianity, Islam, Judaism, Buddhism and Hinduism, and about life-seeing humanism, freedom of faith and religious criticism, the subject will contribute to dialogue on the beliefs, outlook and respect for the life-view of others. The subject is also important because it addresses fundamental philosophical and ethical questions and reflects on the theme related to identity, human dignity, life and death, right and wrong. The RLE subject is a subject where students can discuss issues that are important and difficult in youth: relationships, family, friends, togetherness, homosexuality, sexuality. RLE is a very central subject to meet the school's objective: that students can develop knowledge and attitudes to master both social life and their own life, that students should learn to think critically and act ethically, that education should promote learning and creation and counteract all forms of discrimination. When you were going to change an important subject like RLE, how would you go about doing that? Perhaps a start should be made by asking teachers, elephants and academics about how the subject works, and engaging in dialogue with them to hear about something that could be done differently – and making the subject more relevant, up-to-date and stronger in collaboration with these environments. The way the government and the Minister of Knowledge deal with the RLE subject is almost unbelievable. I hope, for God's sake, that this is the last time that we in Stortinget have witnessed such a lack of knowledge about a subject in school. The government will introduce the K in KRLE and increase Christianity its place in the subject. Those who are going to teach in the subject and who are organized in the Federation of Education and the National Association of Schools say no. No is said by those who are to train the teachers, as the Faculty of Education at the University of Oslo and the Institute of Teacher Education and Education at the University of Tromsø. The students of pedagogics – future teachers – say no. A long line of colleges and universities, such as NTNU, Oslo University and Akershus University and Bergen University, say no. The research environment around RLE – National Professional Council for Christian and Religious Science, Norwegian Religious Educational Research Forum, National Multicultural Training Centre – says no. Parents, represented by the Parent Selection for Primary School, say no. The gender equality and discrimination ombudsman says no. Nevertheless, the Minister of Knowledge takes the initiative and skips all professional advice. It is a completely unacceptable way of driving professional development and disrespectfully towards teachers, researchers and the professional community. The left was actually opposed, but because of the agreement they entered in for this. This is how a game can be played with the students 'heads. This is how a game can be played with subjects in Norwegian school. This means that there are a few very few people who decide which elephants around the country are going to be involved in the timana – people without any professional legitimacy at all. It's just sad. So now there's going to be more Christianity in the school. Then there will be less time for other things, such as ethical reflection, life-vision dialogue and questions of life and death. That change is being made in 2015 in a country with ever-increasing loss of life. It's a priority I think very few young people will understand. And to put it this way: the asso-argument of cultural heritage – no professional bodies have called it the epitome.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1124	There are major changes going on in order to strengthen the basic wall of school, and they are interrelated.
