ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1326	This case concerns changes to the private school law. As the rapporteur on the matter, I would like to begin by thanking the Committee on Cooperation in the examination of this matter. The various parties agree very much on school policy. It is an overall committee that expresses that the public school is and will be the support pillar of the Norwegian school system. But there is also an overall committee that points out that through international conventions Norway has committed itself to the rights of parents and the ability to choose a different school offer for its children from that of the public, and an education that is in line with its own religious, moral and philosophical beliefs. Then there are different opinions in the Committee on some aspects of post-school policy, such as changes in the basis of recognition and changes in the name of the law. I will now prepare for the view of the Christian People's Party on this matter. The parental right is the starting point for the Christian People's Party's free school policy. We believe that we need private schools in Norway as a complement to public school provision. It must be possible to choose an alternative, which is why we are committed to facilitating the conditions of boarding schools and providing them with good and predictable framework conditions. In order for everyone to have a real opportunity to choose an alternative, we are also concerned with a good public funding of primary schools, so that parental pay can be kept at a low level. I am therefore pleased that a majority of the parties are now asking the government to investigate how costs of building can be included in the grant basis of the boarding schools. We know that many hospitals have a tight financial environment, and we know that every fresh school needs good, functional school construction in order to provide students with a good training offer. I am also pleased that the majority is asking the government to look at the possibility of the schools building up a little more equity than they have today, in order to ensure a predictable economy and allow some additional activities related to school activities, such as: cloud arrangements for own students and rental of vacant accommodation. While we want to give schools more flexibility, we want to be crystal clear that we don't want commercial health schools in Norway. School is not an arena suitable for profit and economic gain. These schools are largely run by community funds, and the money is going to benefit students. Therefore, I think it makes sense to clarify the dividend ban and also to extend the transparency of supervision to related companies. The Christian People's Party wants fresh oils, but we don't want any free slip. We made a private school settlement with the red-green parties at Stortinget in 2007, and it formed the basis of today's private school law. But the four parties that agreed in 2007 no longer have a majority in the Stortinget. The Solberg government announced a substantial liberalisation of the private school law. The Health Declaration warned that all those who comply with the quality requirements of the law should have a statutory right to start publicly funded health schools. This policy was not relevant for the Christian People's Party to support. I would therefore commend the Minister of Knowledge for choosing a solution that could give a broader settlement in the Grand Duchy. The government's desire for more diversity is safeguarded by the introduction of two new recognition bases in the law, while continuing the main lines of today's private school law. There will be no law of rights, there will be no right to take up high school. An application for approval still needs to be made, it needs to continue to be applied for on a defined basis in the law, and a discretionary assessment is still required before authorisation is granted. Municipalities will continue to speak and schools will not be approved if this will have a negative impact on public school provision on the ground. I think this is a good solution. It is a solution that implies a still strict law of free school, while opening up some more diversity in the sector than we have today. And I think that's a good solution because it's close to the center. No one is served by a high school policy that is drawn far to the right when there is a blue majority on the Stortinget, and far to the left when the majority is red.

It is unfortunate in a school that is characterized by freedom of method. The major changes in this law relate to what you want to be able to accept as private schools and not. To the dear Christian People's Party – here are five good arguments against the new private school law. There's no shame to turn: It is a strength for Norwegian society that elephants with different social backgrounds, beliefs and culture learn together and from each other in the same public sphere. It contributes to a society with greater understanding, greater trust and stronger community. Multiple elephants in private schools mean fewer children in the common sphere. Experience from Sweden and other countries shows that larger sections of private schools lead to increased segregation. A goal of the law change is to give more innovation to the school. However, an OECD report concludes that the private school market primarily leads to innovations in marketing, not pedagogics. The new law will affect the districts where there is no market for private schools. The choice will increase for some elephants in central districts, but in the districts the offer may be poorer. The need for control will increase, and ensuring that the elephants in new private schools do not circumvent the legislation to take out excess will increase public resources to red tape. In 2005, Norway and Sweden distinguished teams in education policy. Although Røe Isaksen will not introduce a Swedish model, he is now taking a step towards the Swedish border. We don't know how big it's going to be, but we know it's going in the wrong direction.

Then we propose adding two new qualification bases to the law. One is a vocational education programme, and the other is a specific profile, profile schools. To the extent that it involves a release, it is an abuse of the word release. To the extent that it implies – and some claim it – that the door of the door is now wide open, there is strict access control on the way in to the door. Both grounds are defined in the law and in the pre-worker, and in the usual way, as in the current law, further demarcation is carried out by means of authorisation practices. Vocational training schools shall provide training which is consistent with public school training, the host state shall have the right to an opinion, and the views of the vocational training student shall also provide the opinion of the county. The second qualification basis, profile schools, must represent something that is significantly different from what is normal practice in public schools and schools recognised by other foundations of the law. There are essentially two main categories. There are schools that want to pay special attention to a topic, for example: real science, sport, language, art and culture. The other is schools with a different pedagogy from those used in public schools or schools approved on the basis of a recognised pedagogical direction. For the former, the profile schools, it requires more than just glaze on the cake – special professional attention must be given to a subject and individual curricula must be approved to make this visible. So it's not a good idea to just use what's in today's curriculum on the Knowledge Lift.

This is the best guarantee for strong, broad and solid academic environments in schools, which can once again work closely with the different industries. Privatisation will weaken the Norwegian model of working life in vocational and vocational training. The task message ended in a stomach pad, where municipalities are largely entrusted with administrative tasks such as notarial business, sport-functional recognition of swimming pools, the right of marriage of rapporteurs and the management of certain fish species. These proposals largely impressed no one in the municipalities and were largely no real transfer of power to the local authorities. But now, when the government really has the opportunity to open up to local self-government in a matter that is very important to the municipalities, and where local government has first-hand knowledge and knowledge of the issue, no then the government will not let local democracy decide. The Governments of the Right Party and the Advance Party believe that they know better than all the local government together. So in this case, the proposal of the government and the supporting parties is nothing more than a total overrun of the local democracy they themselves say they have strong confidence in. That the municipalities themselves should not get the last word in such an important local policy case as school structure is completely hopeless. This government does not have real confidence in those who represent municipal self-government, and today we are proved beyond any doubt that the government parties with their supporting parties pose a strong threat to local democracy and public schools as a basis in the Norwegian welfare model. At the top of it all, they send the bill to the local government.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1922	I would like to be allowed to thank the proposers behind this proposal for drawing attention to a theme on which we are all concerned. This issue is about how to ensure the admission requirements for teacher training, so that they help recruit good teacher students to future school. At the same time, I would like to thank the Committee on Commitment and Good Cooperation. We all see that there is a clear link between professionally strong teachers and students' learning. Good teacher training is necessary to equip the students to meet the day-to-day meeting them in the classroom. In the Committee, we recognise that there is a link between what students can get into teacher training and what they can do when they go out and finish. I expect the proposers to explain their views, so I will now look into the views of the government parties on the matter. In the Sundvolden Declaration, the government announced that it would increase the recording requirements for learning, in both Norwegian, mathematical and English. And the Teacher Lift, which was launched in the autumn of 2014, says that the government will raise the quality requirement in mathematics from 3 to 4 from higher education. This is a step by step part of the policy announced in the Sundvolden Declaration. As a consequence, the recording regulation has now been amended. From 2016, the entry requirement for primary and teacher education will be 35 school credits, grade 3 in Norwegian and grade 4 in common secondary mathematics. It appears that the proposers for this proposal share the government's intention to strengthen the recording requirements to address the teacher training requirements of today's requirements. I think that is positive. The proposers wish to ask the government to ensure that the grade 3 in Norwegian and mathematics remains as a minimum entry requirement, but at the same time they will ask the government to introduce requirements that applicants for teacher training who do not have the grade 4 or higher in these subjects will pass a compulsory pre-school course in both Norwegian and mathematics in order to qualify for admission. So it looks like they really want more stringent requirements – higher than the grade 3 in Norwegian and mathematics – but with a slightly different set of requirements. Proposalers want two compulsory courses to compensate for the lack of competence beyond what can be expected with the grade 3 from secondary school. I refer to the Government's response letter of 12 May 2015, in which he comments on the proposal for a representative and points out the challenges related to the scope of the proposal and to the winding-up of two sets of advances, and in particular the advances in Norwegian, as well as the impact this could have on the search and admission of the studies. The Council of State stresses in the letter that the Government of the Sundvolden Declaration has set high ambitions for teacher training, with increased admission requirements in the three subjects of Norwegian, math and English, and that the government is now taking the first step in increasing the character requirement in mathematics. In order to compensate for any recruitment failure, an advance in mathematics is added. That's the idea of gradually moving forward, gaining experience and finding good solutions. We all know that the good teacher is not only professionally strong, but also has the ability to learn away, to communicate, to care about children and young people and to be a clear leader in the classroom. It's important both for students' learning and for getting a safe learning environment in school. The challenge is to get the right students to apply for teacher training, to carry it out in a good way and to stay in school. It is therefore exciting that the Committee in this case also makes a proposal for a decision asking the government to facilitate the possibility for the educational institutions that so desire to conduct admission interviews for candidates for teacher training. It could be a good addition to recording requirements related to points and grades. I look forward to a good debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1993	The representative Trond Giske then addressed the proposals he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e1996	In the Sundvolden Declaration, the government announced that it would intensify the admission requirements for access to teacher training. The government's strategy The Teachers' Lift, which was launched last year, says that the government will raise the quality requirement in mathematics for admission to teacher education from grade 3 to grade 4 from higher education. In the first instance, the entry requirement in mathematics is tightened, and it will apply from the autumn of 2016. It's necessary. The Progress Party believes that Director Arild Steen of the Institute for Research at the Institute for Labour was right when, on 26 January, he spoke to the Evening Post: ‘A professional status is also determined by the competence requirements. The more difficult it becomes to enter the study, and the fewer places available, the more the status grows.’ That's part of the solution. A status lifting of the teaching profession is necessary. The teacher is the individual who plays the most important role in the student’s learning. Then we must have confidence in the teachers and ambitions of the teaching profession, the school and the students. The Progress Party believes that enhanced admission requirements for teacher training will be included and ensure that the teacher students have sufficient competence in the basic subjects. Skilled and motivated teachers are the key to a good school. At the same time, the Progress Party will underline the importance of good and attractive teacher training, which is relevant to the tasks and challenges of a newly trained teacher meeting in school. Training institutions are responsible for providing their students with sound professional competence in all disciplines. The extension of primary school teacher education to a five-year master's degree provides good opportunities for the building of the necessary professional skills, as well as for the provision of more practice and more professional immersion. We believe that we need to look at the efforts of the teachers, the Teacher Lift, in a whole. Sharpened admission requirements combined with a new five-year master's education for teachers will in the long run help train more skilled and highly skilled teachers, which is essential for Norwegian students to learn more. That's the main thing: the students have to learn more and better. In this context, the importance of good teachers cannot be stressed enough, but all parties must show will and have ambitions on behalf of knowledge. Norwegian children live in the best country in the world. They are also entitled to receive the best education in the world. The Progress Party will not agree to talk down Norwegian teachers or Norwegian teacher training. On the contrary, we take Norwegian teachers, educational institutions, students and students – the teachers of the future – seriously and take it for granted that they show a willingness to step up their efforts. The effort must be stepped up in all stages. Increased character requirements mean that students need more to get good enough to get into teacher training. In particular, they need to be better in real science, where we are now increasing demands. We must not be so blinded by oil wealth that we believe in national self-righteousness that Norway can meet the welfare challenges of the future by being good at understanding democracy and working together alone. It is the hard subjects, the real ones, that are concretely value-creating and innovation-driven. Norway’s value-generating industries are competence-based and will be even stronger in the future. Oil and gas, renewable energy, maritime, offshore, marine, finance and ICT are value drivers to safeguard the welfare society of the future. These are different industries, but they all have one thing in common: they're based on real science. Increased demands for teacher students are an important building block to ensure future well-being, prosperity and value creation. The party of progress is very pleased that this is now being introduced.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2025	We all agree that we need good teachers in Norwegian school, and we all agree that it is important to recruit the right candidates into the teaching profession and into the teacher training. The government believes that the best solution is to raise the character requirement in mathematics from higher education, from grade 3 to grade 4. That means that someone who dreams of becoming a Norwegian teacher, or someone who has a dream of teaching in social sciences and history, can risk not getting into teacher education because you are not good enough in algebra or are not good enough to solve equations with three unknowns, even though you have good grades, a good diploma and are strong in the subjects in which you want to deepen and teach. I'm afraid we can miss good teachers in the future with such a policy. By raising the math requirement, we secure candidates who are strong in math. But it's probably a rather accurate method of recruiting the best Norwegian talents and the best Norwegian teachers. The proposalers of the Workers' Party have a different approach, but the basic idea is the same. In order to get the right candidates to receive teacher training, it is necessary to raise the character requirements. They have a slightly broader approach. They'll look at both the Norwegian and the math character. They will maintain the requirement for grade 3 in Norwegian and math, but require candidates who are not grade 4 or better to pass a compulsory course before being qualified for admission. I think it makes sense to demand good Norwegian knowledge as to demand good math skills, because good knowledge and skills in Norwegian will have meaning and value no matter what subject you are going to deepen in, and no matter what subject you are going to teach in. I don't think we can say the same thing about mathematics, at least. I believe, in any case, that making more stringent character requirements is the wrong medicine. Being professionally strong is of course important in order to become a good teacher, but good grades are not the only thing to do. Having a six in math doesn't necessarily mean you're going to be a good math teacher. That definitely doesn't mean you're going to be a good Norwegian teacher. Being a good teacher requires a lot more. It requires the ability to communicate, the ability to create a relationship with other people, the ability to communicate and the ability to be a clear leader in a classroom. These are qualities that don't come through a character or a diploma. I am therefore pleased that a joint committee is now asking the government to facilitate the possibility for the educational institutions that want to do so to set up a system of admission interviews. In this way, you will be given a better opportunity to create an impression of the personal characteristics, suitability and motivation of the candidates to go into the teacher’s study. This is not something we want to make compulsory or press the institutions to implement, but we want to facilitate it for those who want it. I believe that the requirement to be admitted to an interview should be 35 points and the grade 3 or better in Norwegian and mathematics. It may well be that there are candidates among the applicants who can be seen to be able to make a good Norwegian teacher, even if you do not have a foursome in math. I would like to take up the proposal of the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and SV, as it appears in the setting. The Christian People's Party will not support the proposal of the Workers' Party. The size has increased the requirement for professional immersion in the field of education, and it is at the level of teacher training that students should acquire the necessary professional knowledge. I think it's completely different things than enhanced character requirements that are needed to raise the status of the teacher's profession and the teacher's training. First of all, I believe that we need to look at the framework of the teaching profession. More time for each student and fewer time thieves are two important keywords to improve the working life of teachers. This will help more teachers to thrive in the job, more teachers to stay in the job, and more teachers to become teachers. Then maybe the character requirement for entering the study will be pushed up in any case, without there being anything we need to adopt politically.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2050	There is a broad political consensus that the competence and suitability of the teacher are the key prerequisites for the quality of teaching. Therefore, teacher training, skills requirements and continuing training have also been on the political agenda for several years. The same applies to the introduction of recording requirements for teacher training. The government has announced what they believe to be more stringent admission requirements from 2016, and the Workers' Party believes that the government's proposal really means removing the requirement for the grade 3 in mathematics, and opening up that everyone with the characters 2 or 3 in mathematics can enter, provided that they complete an advance course. Thus, the requirement for grade 4 is not real. The Central Party believes that today's demands for admission are good enough. We think it's more important what students come out of competence than what they come in with. The teacher’s study shall ensure that the trained teachers meet the requirements of competence and are fit for the profession. Therefore, good quality in education itself is more important than the conduct of symbol policies and the setting of stricter recording requirements. We're more concerned that potentially skilled teachers won't be applying for teacher training because they don't meet the admission requirements in mathematics. Some have used their own misconceptions in mathematics as a resource in the work on the subject, the subject and the students in practice. And we've had students with good grades from high school who only know mathematics at a technical level and can't understand the background well enough to explain mathematics to students." The purpose of education is actually learning. So it's possible to get better in a profession that you've got the grade 3 in in higher education. Printouts show that there will be too few qualified teachers in the years to come. Stricter recording requirements, at least in the short term, could lead to weaker recruitment to the teaching profession. The figures from the department show that 30 pst. of the students in 2014, would not be able to find out if the requirement was grade 4 in mathematics. Finally, we are in favour of the Christian People's Party's proposal to try out recording interviews for the institutions that want it, and we are pleased that the Christian People's Party also believes that today's demands for 3 in Norwegian and mathematics and 35 primary school credits are a good enough starting point to become a good teacher.

There is a balance between high character requirements and recruitment. I am quite convinced that higher character requirements in the long term can also provide better recruitment, simply because of the signal it sends. It is not an assessment of the importance of the subjects, because I fully agree with the representative Giske, who says that Norwegian is a completely crucial and absolutely central subject. It is simply a matter of when political ideals meet the realities of the sector, I believe it is a responsibility to implement it gradually, and to implement it so that we can both ensure higher admission requirements and at the same time not get more recruitment problems than it may look like we have today.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2228	First, I would like to thank the President-in-Office for the good cooperation and presentation of the content of the matter. In addition, we have supported several proposals aimed at antisemitism promoted by the Christian People's Party. Studies in Oslo School in 2011 show that bullying and harassment of students with Jewish faith in the Norwegian school are increasing. Some parents even say that they consider moving from Norway because of the harassment to which their children are exposed. It also emerges that several teachers no longer dare to teach about the Holocaust in a true way – this because teachers fear represals from students who, for various reasons, do not want to be presented with the historical suffering of the Jews. In order to counter antisemitism, it is necessary to work with attitudes both at school and in society. The Progress Party welcomes the response of the Council of State to the Committee, which refers to the possibility of developing teaching materials tailored to teacher training. A global study conducted by the US-based Anti-Defamation League shows that almost half of those who asked had not heard about the extermination of Jews during World War II. Studies show that 15 pst. by the inhabitants of Norway are classified as anti-Semitic. Furthermore, Norwegian men are more than twice as hostile to Jews as Norwegian women, respectively 21 pst. versus 9 pst. This shows that antisemitism is still widespread in Norway and that there is a need to counter these attitudes in society. Knowledge dissemination is thus one of the best means of increasing tolerance. There are several reasons why antisemitism must be taken seriously. One important factor is that Jews, of course, should be treated with respect and met with the same attitudes as others. Another equally important moment is that if we tacitly accept anti-semitism and harassment of Jews, it could form a ground for the formation of more extremist environments in Norway, with the fatal consequences it can have for society, and that is not the way we want it. Norway as a society has a little proud history of relations with the Jews. Perhaps the behaviour of the Norwegian authorities during World War II is most dramatic and shameful. On Sunday 25 October 1942, Chief of State Karl A. Marthinsen sent an oxygen telegram to the country’s police officers and surveyors, and the telegram was stamped ‘secret’. Here it was ordered that all Norwegian Jews be arrested. Personnel from the Nazi state police Germanic SS and from the ordinary police carried out the order quite quickly the next day. The Progress Party is pleased that there is at last a majority in the United Kingdom to draw up an action plan against Jew hatred and antisemitism. We are also pleased that the Ministry will consider various proposals presented in the proposal for a representative, as well as other measures to prevent racism. The Progress Party looks forward to constructive and good cooperation on this.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2371	The core of the ministry's proposal is to open for the grant of fishing rights to a vessel in exchange for the corresponding rights granted to another vessel, without regard to whether the vessel has the same owner. The proposal thus provides for two fleets to enter into a private-law agreement on the transfer of fishing permits without simultaneously transferring the vessel to which the fishing permit is attached. The requirement that it is the fisheries management that allocates the necessary fishing permits to the fishing vessel is fixed. The same applies to current and future exemptions from the activity requirement, as today, to be approved by the Ministry. Thus, such a private law agreement is not binding on the fishing authorities. The amendment also deals with the legal establishment of the applicable rules on the granting of concession, the replacement of vessels and the hall of vessels for continued operation, the splitting and the collection of operational bases. Today this is only allowed in regulations, and the Committee is united in the view that it is important and right to get everything together in a law. At that time, key elements of the concession award will be provided by the Act of Participation. The starting point for the split-up institute is the Ministry’s instructions of 15 January 1999, 7 July 2000 and 26 June 2003, where the purpose was to provide the owners of multi-boat crews with a flexible solution to decide for themselves which vessel the master wishes to fish with at any time his operating basis. Shipowners’ teams must today carry out at least three different application procedures to the fisheries management and, in addition, carry out the purchase and reconnaissance of one of the vessels. To this day, it has our demands for real transactions. There are currently no requirements for the new vessel to be used for the fishing of temporary owners, nor requirements for the duration of temporary ownership. The main objective of the proposal is to simplify this process, where a failure to make a real vessel’s front and back room in order to meet the requirements of the fisheries management. The Committee would point out that the simplification changes that are taking place here do not imply that we are legislating it if someone has circumvented or violated previous legislation. This simplification also does not mean that basic quotas can be split and transferred between vessels, and we stress that recruitment quotas are still not negotiable. What's new is the degree of translatability. But one is still subject to the freedom of discretion of the fishing authorities in order to obtain fishing permits. It is a joint committee that points out that solutions should not be prepared to undermine the new regime. The proposal runs counter to the objective of the Participant Act to increase the profitability and value creation of the industry and to ensure coastal bus services and to benefit the marine resources of the coastal population. SV will therefore oppose the proposed changes and do not support setting up the Committee.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2487	The Government has submitted a legislative proposal on changes to the Participant Act. The central change consists in the so-called splitting of fishing authorisations between two vessels below, irrespective of whether the two vessels have the same owner. The aim is to simplify the process of granting a fishing authorisation, a process that currently appears to be very resource-intensive for all stakeholders. Both fishing industry and management will save significant costs if the proposal is adopted. This is a matter that many fishermen have raised with me, and the proposal also has broad support in the fishing industry. Today it is the case that shipping companies wishing to split fishing permits must carry out at least three application procedures vis-à-vis the fisheries management and also carry out the purchase and resale of one of the vessels. These requirements appear to be meaningless for the parties involved, and there is no reasonable justification in my view that this should be the case. The bill states that only one application will be sufficient, and the purchase and resale of vessels will no longer be necessary. Management deals with such cases annually between 100 and 200, so it is relatively easy to see that the proposal involves significant resource savings. This is simplification in practice. It is important for me to stress that the proposal does not involve changes to the underlying conditions of fishing authorisation. Shipowners will have exactly the same opportunities as before for a new fishing authorisation to be granted to their vessels. It's just the process of reaching this result that's now getting easier. The amendments do not affect any of the basic conditions for ownership of fishing vessels, such as the nationality requirement and the activity requirement. Nor are rules on ownership restrictions, regional bonds, etc. be affected. Fishing authorisations and quotas will continue to be granted by the management and will be linked to a specific vessel with which to fish. The Government has also assessed whether quarantine should be introduced by splitting fishing authorisations in order to avoid circumvention of the legislation. We have come to the conclusion that this is not necessary because existing legislation has provisions that adequately counteract such circumvention. Moreover, a quarantine period would limit the choice of shipping companies in view of the existing legislation, which does not contain any such provision. No one would have earned this. I am therefore pleased that a unanimous committee agrees that solutions should not be prepared to undermine the new regime. The main objective of the proposal is to facilitate very demanding application procedures. However, the proposal also has another positive effect by bringing together all the basic rules on the granting of fishing authorisations under the Participant Act. These are key rules on the sale of fishing vessels for the continued operation and replacement of vessels. However, there are no material changes to these rules, which are currently enshrined in regulations. It is clear that the most basic principles are thus enshrined in the Grand Duchy by a legislative decision and not just in the administrative regulations and practices. The proposal will also contribute to a more transparent and accessible industry fisheries legislation by allowing all the main provisions to be found in the legislation. In this context, the legislative proposal should make a significant contribution to a simpler and more user-friendly fisheries legislation. Too many resources are currently agreeing to unnecessary process work. Instead, resources should be used for more meaningful work in favour of the development of the Norwegian fishing industry and society. I am very pleased that there is an overall committee that agrees with this bill.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2709	I am very pleased that a Joint Nutrition Committee is behind the proposals for decisions in Prop. 118 S on Changes in the State Budget 2015 under the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries – Great Norske Spitsbergen Kulkkompani AS – Lands and Mining. Let me also take the opportunity to thank the Committee for a quick examination of the matter. The background to the proposition is, as many people have had, the demanding situation in the wholly owned subsidiary of Great Norske, the mining company SNSG. As a result of a sharp decrease in the coal price in recent years and a large unrealised loss on foreign exchange insurance contracts, SNSG cannot be operated without the provision of liquidity. On this basis, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Fisheries received, as a major owner in Great Norske, a proposal for a plan of measures from the Management Board on 16 January this year. After careful consideration, the government has now promoted this proposition. The proposal proposes that the State purchase land and infrastructure owned by Stor Norske for 295 million. kr. It is also proposed that a subordinated loan be granted, i.e.: a low-priority loan of 205 million. kr to Great Norwegian. Agreements have been concluded with the group and SNSG's banking relationship DNB on the conditions for the injection of capital, subject to the consent of the Storting. The solution proposed in the proposal implies that the State acquires direct ownership of strategic land and infrastructure on Svalbard, which is currently owned by the Great Norske Group. At the same time, provision is made for further limited coal activities in 2015 and 2016, with the possibility of operation after 2016 if, among others. coal prices are developing positively. The limit of this solution is 500 million. kr. It is higher than the Governing Board requested in its action plan. This is due, inter alia, to: market conditions have deteriorated since the Ministry received the enquiry in January. At the same time, the proposal does not propose to provide the company with the means to develop new business areas, as requested by the board. A concrete business plan should be in place before a decision can be made. Furthermore, the role of SNSK in further business development at Svalbard should be assessed. As indicated by the proposition, the ministry will follow market developments and, of course, the situation in the company closely, and we will appropriately return to the Stortingen on the further development of the group. In addition to the purchase of properties and loans, the proposal suggests that the State redeems all the other shareholders of Great Norwegian in line with share law principles. These shareholders hold a total of 0,06 pst. of the company's shares. Several of them have a long history with the company. However, redemption is appropriate now, as it will provide the State that owns more flexibility in the management of the further development of the Great Norske Group. In assessing this case, I have been concerned that the solution proposed should not weaken the group's basis for further operation. It is therefore envisaged that the group will be given the opportunity to lease back land and infrastructure purchased by the State on commercial terms. Property and infrastructure that may not be leased back may be managed by Great Norske on behalf of the State. There are different considerations relating to the state's ownership in Great Norwegian. The big message of ownership, A diverse and value-creating ownership, shows, among other things: the State’s objective of ownership of the company is to contribute to the maintenance and further development of society in Longyearbyen in a way that supports the overall objectives of Norwegian swallowing policy and that the company operates on a commercial basis. Mining has for many years contributed significantly to stable, full-time Norwegian activity and settlement on Svalbard. At the same time, the company must be run commercially. Aid for coal activities on a non-commercial basis may be unfortunate for a number of reasons, including climate policy. These are considerations that have been emphasised in the proposed solution. It is not to be overlooked that the prospects for coal activities at Svalbard are uncertain. The government will, among other things, therefore come back to Stortinget with its own notification of Svalbard, where the situation of the swallowing society is seen in the light of also the negative development of the mining company.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2807	Thanks to the Committee for a good and thorough examination of the proposal that takes account of changes in the copyright law, where we are now introducing a general contract licence and implementing the EU Heat and Powers Directive. At the same time, we're treating Innst. 341 S to Prop. 126 S, in which we are to approve the consent of the EEA Committee for inclusion in the EEA Agreement of the Directive on certain authorised uses of heat and power plants. The purpose of the latter is to create a common European system for the clearance of so-called ‘hitting plants’ where the copyright holder is unknown or cannot be located. With this directive, we will promote the dissemination of, and thus strengthen, the European cultural heritage. Following the consent of Stortinget, the Committee considers that the clearing of heat plants will be easier for Norwegian cultural heritage institutions as well. As regards the Inst. 328 L to Prop. 69 L, I would like to point out the most important thing in the introduction of a general contract license and the implementation of the Heat and Power Directive in Norwegian law. The Heat Works Directive will be implemented through new rules in the copyright law, and thus we will have a common European clearing system and mutual recognition of heat plants status in all EEA countries. The Committee agrees that such a common approach is needed both for the definition and for the clearing of heat plants. With regard to the general contract licence, the Committee considers that such a system will allow for the clarification of rights in a simple way where individual clearance is impractical or impossible. It is also the Committee's view that such a licence should not be a restriction of copyright but a form of rights management. In the case of both the contract licence and dispute resolution rules, fragmented legislation is currently difficult to obtain. The parties to the government and the Christian People's Party have, in a joint remark on the matter, wanted to make it clear that the purpose of the law is not to restrict access to agreements directly with the rightholder. It is desirable to ensure that the applicable principles for the relationship between individual clearing and clearing under the contract licence are not changed. This also means that the general contract licence shall not cover areas where individual clearance may take place. I would once again like to thank the Committee for a good work, and hope that with today's changes this complex area will appear somewhat less fragmented and inaccessible, so that copyright individuals get what they deserve, while at the same time being able to be sure that Norwegian manufacturing environments continue to produce good Norwegian content, and that distributors and television channels can serve good content in the future.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2820	The special Nordic licensing system has existed for many years and must be able to be said to have been successful and well-functioning. However, existing legislation in this area is fragmented and difficult to obtain. For television distribution, the contract licence rules are today limited to simultaneous and unchanged retransmission. In Denmark, a non-linear service license was granted in 2008, such as: time shifted TV usage, start from the front, week archive, etc. These services have been used by television distributors for several years without a legal framework in place to clear up such rights. This has led to a deterioration in the position of rightholders. A general contract licence will simplify the clearance of rights by also giving effect to a contract concluded with a representative rightholder organisation for rightholders who are not represented by the organisation. The Committee points out that the contract licence is to be considered, in principle, not as a restriction of copyright, but as a form of rights management. With regard to dispute resolution and reflections on the EU Heat and Gas Directive, I refer you to the statements made by the rapporteur and I agree with that. So far, the committee is unanimous, and that is joyous. So I want to present some challenges to the majority, which have an additional comment, which we would like to clarify the significance of. First, the majority writes the following: « the applicable principles for the individual clearance and clearance relationship under the contract licence shall not be changed.» [[...]] What does this mean? It may be harmless, but not if it draws towards not extending the areas of collective management under the new licensing rules. That could overturn the whole balance of the matter. We have heard of examples of distributors offering new additional services without clarifying the rights of such use directly with the artists, perhaps precisely because the law on the general contract license has allowed itself to wait. Furthermore, the majority writes: ‘The majority will emphasise, as stated in the proposal, that the contract licence shall not cover areas where individual clearance can be made. For example, it may apply to areas where there is today a clearing by individual agreements with rightholders or their organisations.’;» This may indicate that the majority does not believe that extended jurisdiction should also mean extended rights to collective management. The question is then: where does it facilitate individual clearance, and how does it work today? We are told that the author/woman is pressed for individual clearance of all rights or agreements claimed by broadcasters and distributors also include new and unknown forms of exploitation, even if the author does not want this. Is that just right for direct clearance? Furthermore, it would be to water out the new scheme if rights were to be able to be cleared back into the value chain in retrospect. If e.g. Norwaco contacts a distributor about the lack of rights clearance, Norwaco has to negotiate with the distributor, not by going back and clearing in retrospect. I would like to challenge the Council of State to confirm in its statement that that is how it should be understood. We've seen through a judge against Norwaco that the concept of broadcasting is being eaten up because broadcasters and distributors without asking the author or Norwaco in advance define broadcasting as broadcasting. As a result, the author is deprived of the right to receive the money for forwarding. In other words, we may risk that the majority's additional comment will lead to a minimum change, even though the Storting is now adopting an extended legal base. A clarification of these questions, I think, is necessary to clarify that there is actually a change in line with the intentions of the message.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2854	I am pleased that a broad majority of the committee supports the government's proposals for changes to the copyright law. The proposal will facilitate the easier use of copyright and other copyrighted material. The legislative proposal has two main elements. The first concerns the scheme described as a contract licence. The general rule is that the use of copyright must be cleared by the individual rightholder. For areas where such clearance is impractical or impossible, we have established legal rules in Norway and the Nordic countries that allow for clearer use – i.e. an agreement – with an organisation representing a substantial part of the rightholders in the field and approved by the Ministry of Culture. This system is called the contract license and has worked well for many years, for example. for the Copier system and for the retransmission of cable broadcasting programmes. Today, the contract licence can only be used in certain areas. The proposal implies an extension of the scheme, so that it applies in general. This is done by the introduction of a general agreement licence provision. If so agreed by the rightholder organisation and the user, they may enter into such an agreement for new uses. However, the assumption is that it is impractical or impossible to clarify the right otherwise than through the use of the contract licence. Similar rules have been introduced in the past both in Denmark and Sweden. This could be good for all actors in this area: users will have legal certainty that all rights have been cleared, the rightholders will receive remuneration for new uses of copyright, and consumers will be able to gain more access to copyright in new services. Certain amendments to the dispute settlement rules for the licence are also proposed. The voting system in the field of broadcasting, the cable dispute subject, is proposed to be repealed, inter alia: in the light of the EU Directive on satellite and cable transmission. In the field of education, it is proposed that disputes relating to agreements relating to the use of copyright in education may be settled by a denominator. This could prevent the copying of copyrights for use in education from being stopped in conflict situations. The second main part of the proposal concerns the implementation of the Heat and Power Directive in Norwegian law. The Directive will address the problems of clearing the digital use of works in libraries where the author or other rightholders of the work are not found. Here I show that in Prop. 126 S for 2014-2015 and the corresponding Innst. 321 S for 2014-2015 has been proposed that the Storting consent to the inclusion of the Heat and Power Directive in the EEA Agreement. The objective of the Heat and Powers Directive is to promote the use of heat and power plants as part of the dissemination and strengthening of European cultural heritage, while safeguarding the interests of rightholders. Cultural heritage institutions across Europe are digitising their collections to create digital libraries. Without a common method of clearing heat plants, this important work will have holes and gaps. Having large libraries available for electronic searches provides better conditions both for researchers and for us common users of cultural heritage. The directive therefore introduces a common European clearing system. The scheme is based on a comprehensive search for rightholders and the subsequent mutual recognition of the inventive step of a work in all EEA countries. The information on the search shall be provided in a pan-European database on heat and power plants, where the National Library is the contact point of Norway. If a rightholder of another foundry was to notify himself at a later date, the work would no longer be a foundry and its use would have to cease. It is proposed that the new identification code should serve as an alternative to the clearing of heat plants under the contract licence, which can be used today to clear up the use of heat plants in the libraries in Norway. The legislative proposal also contains some minor proposals. Among other things, it is proposed to repeal the rules on the expert advice on copyright. The Council has not been in office for many years.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2950	I want to start by thanking the Committee for a good cooperation. In general, the change in the law is about the ability of landowners to determine the spelling of their usernames. We have had consultations on the change and, like in the hearings of the Department, the Committee notes that there is strong opposition from name-finding bodies. The hearing bodies that argue for the amendment of the law believe that the landowner has had too little real impact on issues relating to usernames, although the law today says that the view of the landowner should be taken into account. The hearing bodies that argue against the amendment of the law believe that the usernames are joint ownership and common cultural heritage, not property of a single landowner. In many cases, usernames will lose cultural memory when the owner of the land has to be able to determine its spelling. The founder will be able to choose a writing mode which will support his private surname. The Committee stresses that the use of the term is an important cultural heritage and part of the intangible cultural heritage in Norway. The Committee also stresses that this concerns the use name and not the place name. The Committee shares the view on who shall determine the way in which the use of the name is to be made public. The Labour Party is going to vote against the amendment of the law today on the basis that we want this to be a public task more than the individual's right. Nevertheless, there is reason to point out that the Workers' Party considers that the view of the landowner should be given special emphasis and that the views of the municipalities are taken into account when decisions are taken.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2972	The name is not dubious. It's elementary literacy that we all learned as a child. Our name means a lot to us. It's about belonging, about inheritance, and about something as simple and practical as we all know who we're talking about when we say their name. So it is with the names of places and farms. Fortunately, here we have a good legal protection in our country. The purpose of the Local Name Act is to ‘retain city names as cultural memories, provide them with a form of writing that is practical and appropriate, and contribute to the knowledge and active use of the names’. The public is responsible in this area. The municipality adopts the method of writing the official address and name of the localities, branches, municipal streets, roads, squares, neighbourhoods, etc. The counties determine how the name of county installations is to be written. The spelling of other local names is adopted by the Staten Kartverk. Sometimes, however, disagreements arise, including writing. Two decades ago, Østre Toten travelled the municipality by name about the spelling of a number of local city names. For example, «Hoel» would be written without «e» in one place and «Who» would become «Kvem» in another. For neighbors, for other people in the East Toten and for the State's maps, these things don't mean that much. But it does for those who have got their last name from the farm they live on. Hoel with ‘e’ will not live on Hol without ‘e’, or vice versa. Therefore, he and the 100 other landowners of the East Toten, or some 900 other landowners on a national basis with undecided namesake, should also determine how the name should be written. The Ministry has now taken the necessary steps and amended the law so that landowners can determine the way in which the name is written if this way of writing can be demonstrated to have been in public use in the past. This change is good for several reasons. Firstly, the change is anti-conflict. It delegates responsibilities downwards and does not allow senior agencies to oversee the wishes of the users. This should also be seen as a strengthening of the fundamental rights of ownership. We welcome that in the Right. Secondly, this leads to a strengthening of cultural memory management, which is the very basic intention of the place name law. Cultural memories are best managed through active and continuous use, and this is achieved most effectively by transferring the decision to the person who is actually responsible for cultural memory, namely the owner of the land. Finally, I would also like to express my satisfaction at the fact that the law in this respect underlines the importance of identity and belonging. The law will, after the change, more than before preserve the invisible bond that unites man with his home.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e2991	I will not keep any long posts in this matter. That this is a matter that creates involvement in some environments, there is no doubt about it. Locally, changes in names or new names on roads, ferries and others create an incredible commitment. It is said that in Norway, there is actually a work called ‘Norwegian Gaardnamene’ — at about a point in time. Twenty volumes. Here we find pronunciations, writing forms and interpretations. It's a work that other countries envy us. We choose, together with the Working Party, not to support the proposition. The main argument for the Christian People's Party is that we believe usernames are cultural memories and historical monuments, which belong to the place and community. Family names are private property and must be written as the owner uses them. Incidents, on the other hand, are our joint ownership and must be protected from distortions. A landowner may not have the right to use more than other protected cultural memories. The protection must apply to inherited old names, irrespective of whether the name is a farm name, username or other place name. In the same way as with physical cultural memories, the use names must also be protected against destruction. Protected buildings and objects have legal protection, which must also be of use. Then the spelling must be such that the inherited local pronunciation is used. The purpose of the City Name Act is to preserve the name as a cultural memory. The way you do it, of course, is to use the username, so that you don't forget the name. The public use of usernames requires names to be written down and the law lays down rules on how to write the names. The main principle of the current law is that the spelling of the name should be based on the inherited local pronunciation, and then a written name with a form in Norwegian spelling – a new Norwegian or a book destination – that is closer to the local pronunciation. It is also possible to deviate from this spelling and to choose regional forms that are not available in bookbooks or new Norwegian. Examples of such forms are ‘laundry’ or ‘service’ instead of ‘thear’ or ‘tear’. We believe that the proper use of the name is best regarded as a collective property to be secured by the fact that an expert takes responsibility for the standardisation of the name in consultation with those who have the right to comment on it. A standardisation body must have knowledge and understanding of how to maintain and maintain a username over time. It is on this basis that the Christian People's Party opposes the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3006	The representative Bekkewold was in the work «Norwegian Garden Names» in 19 volumes. I can tell you that the work is missing from the library. For the Centre Party, cultural protection and cultural memory protection are important, both nationally and locally, and often these two dimensions are closely linked. The traditional farm and farm names are among the oldest local names and are recognised as intangible and protective cultural memories. Old geographical names are part of our common cultural heritage and provide direct and uninterrupted links to history, in line with buildings and objects. By continuing to be in use, inherited geographical names are a living legacy from past times. The purpose of the law is to preserve geographical names as cultural memories, as well as to provide them with a method of writing that is practical and appropriate. The state's mapping works have a central function in the administration of the law through the role of decision-making authority for the writing of the names of all mountains, hills, water, rivers, etc. Following the 2005 revision of the law, the cart has been reviewed and taken decisions in many thousands of cases, in line with the law’s requirement that the writing should follow the applicable spelling principles. Many farm names are up to 2,000 years old, and writing has tended to change over time. Many farm names that today serve as family names are written according to the spelling rules of the 1700s and 1800s. And that's where we come to the core of the battle. Many landowners have been surprised in these years when the official method of writing the farm name has been changed without the owners being aware of it. I have been in conversations and meeting both with those who the representative Harberg mentioned, and with the target layer on Toten, so both have been heard. Names for most of us are closely related to our own identity. Sense if this matter is not about the right to determine neither the family name nor the spelling of these, it relates feelings and identity to the way in which the name and the farm name are written. As the law in force has been practised, in several cases, genealogies and farm names have been separated by a new form of writing of the farm name. Many landowners feel that this is an infringement of the identity of their property. Another factor is that in the historical period farms have often been divided into several farms and uses. Therefore, there are often several uses on each farm which are wholly or partly identical to the farm name. Not rarely do the landowners disagree with each other about the type of writing, so that different modes of writing have developed for the original name of the farm of the sub-users. For the centre party, it is important that the bill states that the owner of the law must be able to prove that the method of writing a wish has actually been used as a username. Senses of the current spelling were introduced at a time later than its origin, however, it is of relatively older date and according to date’s writing norm. In such a perspective, there is a historical value in continuing to allow the way of writing from the time when a place name began to be established in written form. These have been incorporated over a few hundred years and are now part of local history. The non-prescriptive nature of a person does not mean that the meaning and meaning of the name are lost. The Central Party understands and respects that there are shared opinions on this bill, but believes that the changes in the law balance the interests of the community and cultural memory and the influence of the landowner on the writing of his own property. The centre party will vote in favour of the proposal on this basis.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3038	Place names are intangible cultural memories. A single geographical name is a peek hole into Norwegian history. A place name can be a little story about how we settled the country, or a glimpse into our ancestors' lifestyle, goals, and faith. The geographical names are also a most living part of our oldest linguistic heritage. They are verbally handed over from generation to generation and best kept in the speech target on the spot. The purpose of the Place Name Act is to protect the geographical names. The range of names shall be continued when they are put into public use. The information that local names can give us about language and culture will be shared by future generations. We're all familiar with hundreds of city names. They're part of our daily life and our local knowledge. We get to know them in growth. They connect us to places, to a branch, to a farm, to a genus. They connect to our last name. Many Nordics have surnames that were originally local names. The legislative proposal seeks to combine two different approaches to site names. It recognises both basic and social interests. Place names have cultural memory for the big community and are thus a common owner. At the same time, we believe that the sense of kinship and personal identity attached to a specific username is a legitimate reason which the law must uphold. The legislative proposal is therefore based on the right of landowners to determine the spelling of the names of their own property and their own farm. The mapping will continue to be a formal decision-making body in the cases. However, if the landowner can prove that the method of writing he wishes on his farm has been in public use as a utility name, the method of writing shall be capable of being adopted without further delay. After the proposal, it will not be necessary for the spelling to follow Norwegian spelling principles, as is the condition of today's law. I am pleased that the committee has noticed that the amendment concerns only the username, i.e. the name of the private property or the farm. The change does not apply to any extensive farm name or other geographical name. The proposal involves a liberalisation that benefits the owner of the land on his small cadastral parcel, but the owner-determined method of writing should not be influenced by the amount of local names for which the municipality, county municipality and state adopt the form of writing. In order to be concrete, if the name of the place of use is to be entered in a street address agreed by the municipality, public standardisation is to be used. We have followed up a position that a unanimous cultural committee gave in 2008. The store asked that the rules on the writing of the username be softened. The task was to change the law so that a username which is identical to a farm name may not be written as the farm name. If a landowner wants to write the username Vik with W, he will be allowed to do so on the basis of the proposal, even if the Cartwork adopts V for the farm name from which the username is derived. If the municipality will adopt the street name Viksvejen, it will be written with V, as we otherwise write the Norwegian word «vik». The way of writing conveys directly what the word means. Official standardisation is therefore still required when state and local authorities make their write-down decisions. Place names should still have a way of writing that does not help to hide the linguistic origin or what the name tells us about nature and human activity. Local name management, e.g. in map production, has an important additional practical dimension for cultural memory management. We need a secure location of locations and events. It's e.g. important in an emergency context. For these reasons, the Mapping Board should continue to be a formal decision-making body for the use names. The vast majority of local names to be used by the public on maps and signs already exist on the population mouth. However, local names often require a write-down decision when the name is to be used by the public. Therefore, the Place Name Act is a completely real tradition bearer on behalf of all of us. Many geographical names would have been lost if the law had not ensured a continuation. When the landowners have a greater impact on the continuation of the geographical names, we give them confidence and responsibility as traditional bearers. That is why our proposal is that they should also be able to ask for a name-based recommendation if they so wish. I hope and believe that the legislative amendment proposal leads to fewer conflicts between the decision-making body and the founders. It is good for justice and builds up unnecessary bureaucracy. At the same time, I hope that understanding of why the place names need management as inalienable cultural memories can be strengthened.
