ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3133	The first speaker is Mr Mette Tønder, who is now acting as rapporteur on matters.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3136	First, I would like to thank the Committee for its good work on this matter. There has been a case where there has been agreement within the committee, agreement that we should ratify, and agreement on how to implement the Convention in Norwegian law. Children may be associated with several countries. A child may be a national of one country, live in another, live in a third country, and different rules of law and lack of cross-border communication make it more difficult for these children to receive assistance if they, for example: be subjected to care failure or abducted by their parents. It is therefore important to provide these children with increased protection and legal certainty. 41 countries have so far acceded to the Convention, including the EU Member States. Therefore, it was now high time that Norway too took this step. The Convention is proposed to be implemented by incorporation. This means that the Convention is applicable as Norwegian law. A separate law on the implementation of the Convention is proposed. In addition, amendments to the Child Protection Act, the Marriage Act and the Children’s Act are proposed in order to adapt to the Convention. The Convention regulates child protection measures. It concerns parental responsibility, permanent residence, association, guardianship, place in foster homes and institutions and the management of the child's wealth. The Committee has chosen to make some comments on some of the amendments. In order to cooperate with other countries, provision has been made for the transfer of personal data. Some of the hearing bodies have commented on this. This is explained in the proposition. This refers to the provisions of the Convention requiring personal data to be processed confidentially and used only for the purpose to which the data relate. Furthermore, the information shall not be provided if it would endanger the child or its wealth or pose a threat to the child’s family. The essential importance of the Convention is best for the child, and this may justify the fact that also minor threats or other adverse consequences prevent the disclosure of information. The jurisdictional provisions are amended to the place of usual residence of the child. This means, for example: that parents cannot avoid a child protection measure by taking the child out of the country if the child is normally resident in Norway. Parents who do not agree when moving out of the country are given the opportunity to bring a case before the court. Children's right to be heard in matters of movement is strengthened. It was discussed whether the age limit for children’s right of participation was to be 12 years or 15 years, and the Committee considered that it was a right decision to give children participation from 12 years of age. A provision is introduced to allow voluntary foster home and institution placement in other Conventional countries for children living abroad but who have normal residence in Norway. The good follow-up of Norwegian authorities to such locations is limited and there is support for restrictive practices. The purpose of the Hague Convention is to provide greater protection for children in international situations and to enhance international cooperation. We adults are moving more across borders now than what we did just a few decades ago. This means that more children are also more international. It is important to have cooperation between countries so that these children do not become throwing balls between different systems that do not talk. The ratification of the Convention will provide Norway with a new instrument for the prevention and resolution of international parental, child protection and child abduction cases.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3157	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office, for a good cooperation within the Committee and a good, complete dossier. With regard to the ratification of the Convention, it is good that it should be put in place, and the President-in-Office has given a good start on it. I just want to say that the Convention is important and necessary, especially in relation to child Abduction. However, I would like to link some comments to the changes. It is important to know that this Convention does not concern the determination of danger and fun. So the introduction of the Convention does not have a direct impact on the transfer or regulation of parents, but it regulates the parental authority. The question at issue in this connection is surrogati. So this case does not apply to surrogati, but it is shown that foreign surrogati contracts and decisions are contractual relationships that do not necessarily refer to parenting and parenting. It is worth noting that the states that have acceded to the Convention are also not countries that offer commercial surrogati to foreign citizens. The law clarifies what is a common parental responsibility. This is important in order to clarify that both parents are required to consent to e.g.: shorter holiday trips. It also clarifies that consent should be obtained by extending the stay and by making changes if you have agreed to move – which is quite relevant with today’s removal pattern. Paragraph 40 of the Children’s Act states that parents cannot move children out of the country where there is disagreement, and the case is not ‘decided’ and enforceable. It is important in view of child abductions and ensuring the best for children. There have been a number of issues in the media in recent times where someone wants to leave the child’s place of residence or belonging excluded from child protection and care in Norway, for example. by complying with the laws of their own countries – cf. The criticism that has come from other countries. So it's not like that. § 1-2 of the Act on the Protection of Children provides for the right of child protection to intervene when children are not properly cared for and the child is in the kingdom. Nor does the law require that the child be resident in Norway — and this applies regardless of the duration of the stay — if there are questions of care assumption or urgent decision. The law also applies for short stays abroad, and it is important for us that the law applies equally to all children and to the best of the child. As the rapporteur pointed out, the amendment of the law also means that parents cannot avoid a child protection decision by taking the child out of the country, if their usual residence is Norway. This is necessary in order for the protection of children to be able to follow up and implement the measures they have taken. Many children live in other countries, but usually live in Norway. The change in § 1-2 allows the Child Welfare Service to examine concerns that come with these – something that is positive and good for children who are not doing well abroad, which we unfortunately know is too often happening. The change in Section 1-2 also implies that the county council will take decisions on care and behaviour at the institution, even if the child has left the country but is considered to have a normal residence in Norway. The fact that placement decisions can be made means that children can be assured even if they are living abroad and that they receive the necessary follow-up and protection that we believe is important. These are important measures that will provide the best for children. Then we can follow up the kids if they're in the country, or if they're abroad. It has been assessed whether a specific provision should be made in Chapter 8 of the Children’s Act concerning the municipality responsible for children living abroad who are normally resident in Norway. The Committee took note of this. It is important that it is done and that the municipalities know what their responsibility is. I hope that the Council of State will follow up on this as well. The amendments to the laws and accession to the Convention will help to improve the protection of children, and the Workers' Party supports these changes.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3185	I am pleased that today the Stortinge deals with the proposition with the proposal that Norway now finally joins the Hague Convention in 1996. The Convention will increase the protection of children linked to several countries and will strengthen cooperation with other countries in parental, child protection and, in particular, child abduction cases. More and more children are linked to more countries and it may be extra demanding to protect them. A child may be a national of one country, live in another country and reside in a third country. In recent years, both ministries and foreign stations have been increasingly active by foreign authorities, especially in the area of child protection, where they are asked to intervene or resolve individual cases. Without the right means, this can be difficult. The accession to this Convention means that international parental rights and child protection matters can be better prevented and resolved by Norwegian authorities, in good dialogue with foreign authorities. The Convention regulates private international law and cooperation with regard to parental authority and child protection measures. The Convention shall apply in matters of parental responsibility, permanent residence, association, employment, place of residence and institution and the management of the child's property. A key objective is to prevent child abortion. Norway is a member of the 1980 Hague Convention on Child Abduction. The 1996 and 1980 Conventions are intended to work together. The Hague Convention 1996 can help prevent child abduction cases and provide a stable care solution after a return. The Convention provides that the country in which the child is normally resident may take safeguard measures. However, the country in which the child resides may take temporary and urgent measures. A case may be transferred to another country when it is at the best of the child. The Convention also has rules on the choice of law and rules on the recognition and implementation of protective measures taken in other countries. The Convention further facilitates cooperation and exchange of information between countries. The Convention requires each country to establish a central authority to cooperate with the central authorities of other Convention countries, to provide guidance on the Convention and to provide information on laws and services. In order for Norway to accede to this Convention, it is necessary to make some legislative changes. Some amendments are also desirable in order to facilitate the proper adaptation of Norwegian law to the Convention and its objectives. The government therefore proposes in its proposal amendments to the Children’s Act to prevent child Abduction and to prevent children from being left abroad. When proposing amendments to the Children’s Act, it is also considered that it is a central objective of the The Hague Convention 1996 to facilitate the continued association and contact of parents and children, even if the child and the other parent move to another country. We propose, among other things: parents who do not agree are given the opportunity to bring their own action before the Court of Justice to move the child out of the country. It is also proposed that children over 12 years of age should consent to move abroad on their own. This is particularly important in preventing coercive or sexual mutilation. We know that this often happens through strong coercion from the parents, and without the child really knowing what's going on. Today, child protection can only take action when the child is living in Norway. It is also proposed to include in the Child Protection Act that measures can be taken when the child is normally resident in Norway. This is necessary in order for the Child Protection Act to comply with the Convention. It also means that decisions on care can be taken when a child is normally resident in Norway, even if the child is living abroad. The proposal may prevent parents from evading a child protection decision by travelling from Norway. The government also proposes to open up the possibility of voluntary foster home and institution placement in other Convention countries. It then requires that parents and children over 12 years of age consent to the accommodation and that the accommodation be sound and in the best interests of the child. It is high time that Norway took part in this international cooperation. 41 States are now members of the Hague Convention 1996, including the EU Member States. The government has prioritised its work on this proposition. I am pleased that the Stortinget today supports Norway's agreement to the 1996 Hague Convention, which will help to increase the protection of children associated with several countries.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3349	Child protection and child protection issues are complicated. Often we hear about episodes where we get in too early and about other episodes where we get too late. Therefore, it is as important to focus on prevention and early efforts to help children as early as possible in the family where possible. That is why I am very pleased that the Stortinge today is treating Prop. 72 L with proposals for amendments to the Children’s Act, to protect children and to provide child protection with more tools to help children much earlier. Proposition is a follow-up to NOU 2012: 5 Better protection of children’s development. It also follows from the Sundvolden Declaration that there is a need for greater access for the protection of children to provide the necessary assistance, even without consent, where this is necessary in order to prevent the taking-over of care. With this amendment, child protection services are given the opportunity to enter the home with earlier help even in cases where parents do not want help. It is consistent with the starting point of the Child Protection Act. The principle of law is that children should grow up with their biological parents and that assistance under the Child Protection Act should be provided primarily at home. Today, child protection services have few instruments if parents do not agree to relief measures and the situation is not so serious that there is a need for care. In recent years, several professional settings and former child-restraints have also expressed the fact that child-rescue assistance is often too late. The proposals adopted today will improve the legal certainty of children and help children to receive the necessary assistance and care in a timely manner in accordance with the Child Protection Act. The aim of the proposal is to improve the situation of the child and to prevent more interventional measures such as taking care of the child. I am very pleased that an overall committee and a total pool are concerned with preventing the need for care-taking, where possible. Taking care is a very interventional measure. However, I am also pleased that several hearing bodies have pointed out that the amendment of the law may mean that drastic interventions such as care takeover can be avoided. Extending access to the imposition of relief measures will help to establish adequate and effective relief measures in a timely manner, even if the parents oppose them. The proposal allows child protection to impose on three new main categories of action: compensatory measures, designed to reduce the burden on the child of caring measures, which shall help parents to provide care for control measures, which shall verify that children are not subjected to abuse or abuse; In addition, it is also proposed to relax the conditions under which home supervision is to be imposed and it is proposed that it should be possible to impose such measures. Adding the decision to the independent county council will help ensure legal certainty for children and parents. It is important that the child protection service ensures that children are also adequately consulted and involved in these matters. The involvement of children, as also mentioned by the Committee, is an important part of the process. The child’s right of participation is clearly governed by the Child Protection Act and must be an integral part of the child protection process. I have noticed that the majority of the Committee believes that the list of compensatory measures should not be exhaustive, but that it should be opened up to impose ‘other similar compensatory measures’. I still believe that, for reasons of legal certainty, the legislative text should make clear which measures can be implemented. At the same time, I understand that the Committee considers it appropriate that the list is not exhaustive, given that the use of measures in the field of child protection is constantly changing. I live well with that majority proposal if it can also lead to more children getting help at the right time. As regards residence in parents and children’s centres, the Committee has proposed that this be imposed under the same conditions as for other care-changing measures and control measures, i.e. ‘When necessary to ensure adequate care for the child’. The conditions proposed by the Committee are not as strict as the conditions proposed in the proposal, since the proposal proposes that the conditions for taking care must be met in order to impose residence in centres for parents and children. Orders to stay in parental and child centres mean that both parents and children are forced to leave home. In addition to the fact that it is very intrusive to impose some stays away from home, it may also be stressful to be under constant observation. Nevertheless, in this case, I understand the reasons contained in the Committee's comments, and it is good that we also see that staying in parental and child centres can be a preventive measure and not just an action.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e3619	This case is well-established and in line with political leadership – both the pension agreement at Stortinget, which the government parties are behind, and the Sundvolden declaration. The matter today is about two things. One thing is that the basic amount of the population's money is increased from 88 370 kroner to 90 068 kroner from 1 May 2015, which represents an increase of 1.92 pst. The same increase will also apply to disabled persons and others who receive pensions and benefits regulated by changes in the basic amount. I have realized that no one has disagreed with the size of the basic amount for 2015. In the case of an old-age pension during payment, this shall be regulated as usual, with an average of the price and salary increases. This is done in practice by regulating the pension corresponding to the salary increase and deducting a fixed factor of 0,75 pst. The 2015 wage increase is expected to be 2.7 pst., as the government estimates in the revised national budget for 2015. Age pensioners have not yet experienced a negative trend in purchasing power. This year there has been a very moderate wage settlement, and the reality in very many jobs is at best zero settlement. We have thousands who are now losing their jobs, even more people are at risk of losing their jobs, and many are on leave. The right has great respect for the model in which we have discussions with different parties before the government takes its decision and submits it to the Stortinget for final decision. I do not know that the Stortinget has changed the government's proposal in the social security schemes here in the storeroom. There has been a strong contribution to the fact that it has always been able to discuss with a number of governments with the various organisations the determination of the government, becoming a majority for and little political noise out of it. It is, in my opinion, a very good model, which is well suited to both the front-line model of Norwegian working life, which is premissive of wage making, and the tripartite cooperation. It would be very demanding if a practice were adopted whereby the treatment of social security settlements, the setting of the basic amount and old-age pensions led to a political debate at the same time as discussions are taking place. It helps to weaken the discussion institute and the possibility of stable framework conditions. The 2015 estimates show that the estimate of wage growth, price growth and the 0.75 rule indicate that current old-age pensions can have a real decrease of 0.15 pst. this year. There is some uncertainty about this because the final figures are not clear until the price increase is clear when 2015 is over. So we can actually have a discussion here that in practice is not real when we get to the end of the year 2015. Against this background, the Right believes that it makes sense to first look at developments in 2015 and then work in parallel with the case in order to find mechanisms that in the future will not result in a reduction in purchasing power for many consecutive years if employees gain purchasing power over the same period. The right is satisfied that both the Council of State and the Stortinget clarify that such a review should not weaken the sustainability of the pension system in Norway. Nor can there be any guarantee against a decrease in purchasing power in some years and any deviation between expected and actual wage growth in the last two years, which is common practice. The review proposed by the government is now supported unanimously by the Stortinget. I'm happy about that. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e4129	Let me first say that this is a committee with a good working environment. We're working on an area where the discussions are often polarized. Nevertheless, we cooperate well and with good tone at the end of the setting. It must be said that for different reasons the setting does not look exactly as it should have done in the case of minority proposals. The Secretariat and the Committee Secretary took this very seriously. We have received service and assistance in large quantities. We are grateful for that. So to the matter. Some people count the success of a welfare scheme according to its cost, how many are on it, or how high a chance it has. We don't. Success in a welfare system depends on how many people it contributes to helping back to work, to self-sufficiency. Here lies the fundamental difference in the view of well-being between the right and the left. That's why the left-hand side calls the right-hand side unsocial. It's not a description of real intentions or commitment, although it might seem like they think so. It comes from the fact that the right-hand side of one scheme will combine different instruments, so that the social benefits together become a good scheme. The transitional benefit has helped many, but for some it has also become a challenge. Until 1998, the transitional aid was of ten years' duration. Most of the parties at Stortinget then agreed that it was too long, and it was reduced from ten to three years. The Right Party and the Working Party agreed on that change. The Rikstrøgdeverket evaluated the change in 2004 and came, among other things: until: ‘For the social marginal group, the loss of transitional benefit meant the doubling of the proportion of persons employed.’ There was a debate on this. Would this improve the system, or would poverty be at risk? That made the system better. Later, the transitional aid has been adjusted in several ways. The conditions have been changed, made clearer – and some say more stringent. The result was that more people came to work faster. Today, there is a majority for all the government's proposals, with the exception of one. I mean overall that we're doing better. In a little bit of different constellations on different suggestions, we make adjustments in line with what we've done in the past. Nothing combats poverty like having a job to go to. That should be the basis for anyone who wants to have a successful social policy, and that is the basis for the proposed changes. We ensure that the education taken is relevant to getting a job. Here, Nav has an important guidance task, but it is true that this is a requirement, in order to prevent longer social benefits than is necessary. We clarify Nav's guidance responsibility, precisely because we know how important close follow-up is. As a continuation of the general rule, that of staying in Norway, we reduce the possibility of receiving money abroad to six weeks. This is today of six months, much longer than is possible for the vast majority of others to leave the country in connection with vacations. It underlines the importance of being in Norway to have good follow-up, and reinforces the ability to monitor Nav. It brings me to the next point: the possibilities of control are strengthened. Between January and April, there has been a report of social security fraud to a lone mother or father of 14 million. kr. It is important that the rules are simple to understand, easy to follow and easy to manage. So let's do something about that. It's no secret that I wish we could also do something about the age limit. In the context of the very positive effects adjustments in terms and duration have had in the past, namely that it has helped people faster in work, I believe the proposal is good and important. That's why the transition benefit is so good, and that's why it's so important, because it's just going to give financial support to single-parents in periods when you need a job or training. We're proud of the transitional support that we helped shape in our government period, and that's because we know that it works, and that it's accurate. More than nine out of ten recipients are women, and when the scheme is tightened up, everyday life becomes tougher for many single mothers, and we risk more kids becoming in poverty. All relationships in life are not planned. Crime and disease are examples of it – it’s independent of your own or your child’s age. If you stay alone and have a job like this. requires a lot of evening work; yes, the situation can be that life does not go on. Therefore, SV believes that single-parents should have the right to receive support for education, even in cases where re-training is required in order to get a job with a working time appropriate to the opening time of kindergarten. People should have the opportunity to qualify for a safe job, which can be combined with the very life situation that lives alone. I would also like to thank the case rapporteur, not necessarily for being so politically mobile, but for being very helpful with the short deadline we have had. The case rapporteur has previously accused SV of being against everything. That's not right. We support a great many of the changes that actually lie here – the changes in the labour market law, the simplifications in the social security law, the fact that there is civil status to determine the entrance to the social security system, the need to have clearer rules for Nav's responsibilities, and not least the need to introduce sanctions rules and common rules for labour-related measures, as well as provisions relating to child-care. What we do not support, and what we vote against, are the rules that do not take into account real life. That is why we are opposed to limiting access to payment on a foreign basis. We think it's enough with the activity obligation and what's there. We are opposed to the loss of the transitional benefit from the first day with new cohabitation and want primarily to maintain today's legislation. Secondly, we will vote in favour of the Labour Party's proposal. And of course, we're opposed to changing the age limit from eight to six years and lifting the exception rule for children's age in the event of a breakup. Finally, I would like to mention that we are going to vote against the new distributed proposal, proposal No. 5th@item: inlistbox The left had a good reason for that. I have to admit that there is neither cohabitation nor marriage that makes children.

In these three counties, the unemployment rate has increased by 49, 19 and 16 pst respectively. In total, this represents 4,100 people, who are mostly involved in oil-related work. After the employer's period, the government assumes compensation of employees, but the daily allowance is only 62.4 p.m., so there is also an expense for the individual employee. The Communication makes it clear that the Right and Advance Party in the Government have a clear objective of facilitating healthy and active ageing. It affects several parts of our society. Among other things, this means facilitating the possibility for older people to work longer. Life expectancy has increased over the last hundred years, people have improved their economy, better living conditions, better opportunities for education and access to healthcare. There are therefore many who have the opportunity to continue to contribute to society, even after reaching retirement age — and more people simply want to stay longer in work. When we talk about the elderly, we often talk about elderly caregivers with care needs and the need for health care. Healthy elderly people — those who still have their health and will, their desire and their ability to participate in working and social life — are easy to forget. I think we need to change that. A sustainable Norway we get when we use all forces. It is also a fact that in the coming years there will be an increasing need for health professionals. It is therefore important to use the valuable labour that a significant proportion of health professionals over 75 years of age make up. I also think it is a great advantage that more patients will have the opportunity to keep their doctor longer, the doctor who has known the medical history of the patient over decades, who knows family and social conditions – and who not least enjoys the full trust of their patients. Many patients will be pleased to keep a faithful, competent and professionally fully up-to-date doctor longer, even though he or she is both gray in hair and born around 1940. I also notice that this age limit is curious. It is distinct in the European context. I see no need for Norway, with a great future need for health professionals, to stick to such special restrictions. Nor is this age limit based on evidence that older doctors and other health professionals pose a greater risk to their patients than their younger colleagues do. The party of progress has long been a champion to remove the age limit for licensing doctors. In 2010, we promoted a proposal for a representative on this subject. This is supported by the Medical Association, which believes: ‘Age limit is less suitable as a means of protecting patients from malfunctioning elderly doctors. either due to age impairment, illness or professional inactivity.’;» [[...]] I am glad that today we also clarify the conditions for authorisation. The authorisation system is important to ensure that we have authorised our healthcare personnel. Today, more education abroad takes place, and more move to Norway to work. It is therefore essential that the SAK, the National Agency for the Authorisation of Health Care Professionals, also carry out a work that ensures, as far as possible, that we have available qualified labour in a resource-efficient way, because the need for health professionals will be increasing in the future. Today, we are taking a significant step in the right direction to give qualified health professionals the opportunity to work longer. I'm very happy about that.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e4743	Our health and care services must have the confidence of the population. It requires quality, patient safety and safety at all stages. This is also true in the issue of raising the age limit and in the question of whether we should at all have an age limit for the withdrawal of authorisation. First to the general age limit question: Many countries do not have age limits for health professionals, but recertification schemes involving suitability assessment. Norway does not have such recertification schemes. The Ministry points out that, overall, a general recertification scheme would involve a large number of recertifications and would therefore be very demanding and costly. I note that all parties in today's cases will maintain the age limit principle, although a certain age limit is always demanding, as there are always large individual differences. The working party believes that an age limit for the withdrawal of health professionals’ authorisation is set at the right level, combined with an opportunity to apply for a licence — which precisely ensures individual differences when reaching old age — is a scheme necessary to achieve an objective. At the same time, there is a system which is not disproportionate to those covered by it. There are 15 agencies that oppose the proposal to raise the age limit to 80. Several of these agencies believe today's system works well, and the Norwegian nurses' association says among other things. the following: ‘In order to balance the interests of patients’ demands for competent and sound assistance against the burden of health professionals by having to apply for a licence, we believe that the patient’s interest must go ahead.’ It is equally important for the patient’s confidence in the Norwegian healthcare system to set the age limit for withdrawal of authorisation to 75 years as it is for the safety of Norwegian road drivers to require drivers over 75 years of age to have a medical certificate and to apply for the retention of their driving licence. It therefore appears very strange and as a clear discrimination when government parties, Left and SV want one rule to practice as a doctor and another to practice as a driver. So the same person will be able to operate a life-threatening damaged patient in an operating room, but at the same time they refuse to drive a car. Even in 2010, the Medical Association supported an age limit of 75 years and a practical driving licence test when the age limit for driving licences was raised from 70 years. For the Workers' Party, it is very important to stress that the age limit of today of 75 years is not absolute and does not imply any occupational ban. On the contrary, it is very gratifying that more and more applicants for a licence after 75 years of age. Older health professionals constitute a significant resource with a comprehensive and sound health professional background and experience that should be exploited. For older workers, the threshold for participation in the labour market should be low. Therefore, we ask the government to look at schemes to simplify the process of applying for the renewal of health care professionals’ authorisation after the age of 75. Despite large individual differences and improving general health in the population, the Labour Party does not find sufficient evidence and strong enough arguments to raise the age limit for withdrawal of health professionals up to 80 years. The general age limit in working life is now 72 years old, and the special age limit for health professionals of 75 years is already high. The Labour Party therefore does not support the Government’s proposal and, for the sake of order, would draw attention to the fact that, in the case of Prop. 74 L, the Labour Party will vote against I with the exception of the first paragraph of Section 74. So we're asking for a separate vote on this point. Otherwise, we vote in favour of II, III and IV. In the case of Prop. 99 L, the Labour Party will vote for I, except for the second paragraph of Section 48a, where we support the minority proposal of the Committee. The Labour Party shall also vote in this case in respect of II, III and IV.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-11.ana.ud2516e4852	The age limit of 75 years for the lapse of authorisation with the possibility of applying for a licence after that is perceived as a significant threshold among some groups of older people who wish to continue in the profession. We get older, but we have better health, and we live longer. Life expectancy has increased steadily since the age limit of 75 was introduced by the Medical Act, and older people remain functionally healthy. At the same time, technological developments help to make it easier to remain professionally up-to-date and to follow up the patient.
